## A Letter to the Translator.

---

Do not worry, my dear Sir <note>original: "m. H." (mein Herr), a formal address to a gentleman</note>, that your translation of the *Apologues* <term>Apologues: short moral fables or allegories, usually intended to convey a philosophical or ethical lesson</term> of the meritorious Johann Valentin Andreä <note>Johann Valentin Andreä (1586–1654) was a prominent German theologian and writer, famously associated with the Rosicrucian manifestos and social reform</note> might stand in the way of the small monument that I have promised to raise to him from his own writings. It was with no other intention that I sought to refresh his memory—and therefore scattered poems, fables, and dialogues of his here and there—than that the attention of good people might be directed toward him, and that our own age might come to know the man who, in his century, bloomed like a **rose among thorns**. It can, therefore, be nothing other than most welcome to me if another

<summary>In this introductory letter, the writer encourages the translator to publish the works of Johann Valentin Andreä, describing Andreä as a rare intellectual beauty who stood out in a difficult century.</summary>

does <note>Continuing from the previous page: "...if another [does]"</note> what I could not yet do: for the time for the monument, as I had it in mind, has not yet arrived, and every introduction to the spirit of this amiable man prepares the way for that desirable time.

I was even more delighted, however, when I saw from the first six printed sheets of your translation that you had conceived the bold, philanthropic <note>original: "menschenfreundlich," meaning a love for humanity or benevolent intent</note> idea to present your author not only *to* our age, but *for* our age—to dedicate him to it, as it were, through the selection and adaptation <note>original: "Umkleidung," literally "re-clothing," implying a stylistic update to make the old text accessible to modern readers</note> of his finest pieces, in the manner that our age might view and require him.

To translate Valentin Andreä is truly no small feat, and I know of almost no older writer who

V

a 3

<meta>catchword: also</meta>

...who occasionally made the work more difficult for the translator. His style of writing is a delicate web of allusions—partly to books that he read, partly to the affairs that he saw and conducted, and partly to characters and the secret spirit of his age, which he understood with piercing insight. Just as it is much easier to bring to light general truths and speculations (which perhaps seem to belong to all ages precisely because they are suited to none) than those subtle, individual observations that spring from innermost feeling and from the intuitive contemplation of the spirit of things around us, so this work becomes even more difficult in the style that Andreä chose. With him, everything becomes allegory <note>original: "Einkleidung," literally "clothing" or "dressing up" an idea in a literary form</note> and poetry; his wit strikes **subtly**, but

<summary>The author describes Johann Valentin Andreä’s complex literary style, noting that his deep personal allusions and intuitive observations make him far more difficult to translate than authors who deal only in generalities.</summary>
<keywords>Johann Valentin Andreä, translation challenges, literary style, allegory, historical allusions, intuition</keywords>

--- Page 12 ---
<meta>This page continues from page V, concluding the thought that Andreä’s wit is as fleeting as sunlight, which poses a significant challenge for a translator who must avoid the temptation to over-explain his dense, poetic imagery.</meta>

also fleeting, like a ray of sunlight: the lightest garment is always what his ethereal figures love most. Just as explaining and paraphrasing are not his way, he allows them just as little to his translator. This translator must strive to match Andreä's artistry, making an ingenious little poem—conceived in the sharpest outlines—as vivid for the present day as it perhaps was (and was intended to be) for only a few people in Andreä's own time.

Moreover, Andreä lived in times that were not free from Gothic taste <note>original: "gothischen Geschmack"; here referring to the ornate, complex, and highly decorative literary style of the 17th century</note>; indeed, it was a time in which this taste showed itself in the most seductive manner. The modern languages whose literature he especially loved were Italian and Spanish; but it was precisely the most famous writers of these lan-

<meta>This page continues the critique of Johann Valentin Andreä’s literary style. While the previous page discussed the influence of Italian and Spanish literature, this page addresses the broader "Gothic" or Baroque tendencies of the 17th century that prioritized ornamentation over classical simplicity.</meta>

VII
a 4

Languages at that time overflowed with that "sweet foam" <note>original: "süssen Schaum"; a metaphor for the ornate, flowery, and often superficial Baroque prose of the 17th century</note> which could be called the taste of the seventeenth century—and which, one might hope, remains unique to that era alone. From this influence, our Andreä was also not entirely free. You must have often felt, while translating, how many of the refinements of his style become mere subtilties <term>subtilities: from "Subtilitaten," referring to overly intricate or clever rhetorical points that risk becoming obscure</term> or overloaded ornamentation.

His manner is ingenious: he says much with few words; however, within the confines of a narrow framing <term>framing: from "Einkleidung," the literary device of wrapping a moral or philosophical message within a fictional shell or dialogue</term>, he attempts to say far too much with far too little. Furthermore, because these brief framing works date to his younger years, and his busy mind never found the leisure to polish them according to the rules of ancient Greek or Roman simplicity <note>original: "Simplicität"; the classical ideal of clarity and restraint in writing, which stood in contrast to the decorative Baroque style</note>, his dialogues certainly fall behind those of Erasmus <note>Desiderius Erasmus (1466–1536), the famous Dutch humanist whose "Colloquies" were celebrated as the gold standard for pure and elegant Latin prose</note> in terms of purity of style—

<summary>The author critiques Andreä’s early writings for their dense, ornate Baroque style, noting that his busy life prevented him from refining his work to match the classical simplicity of Greek, Roman, or Erasmian models.</summary>
<keywords>Johann Valentin Andreä, 17th-century style, Baroque literature, literary framing, Erasmus, classical simplicity, translation challenges</keywords>

VIII

[if we] were to speak, his apologues <term>apologues: moral fables or allegorical stories intended to convey a lesson</term> rank behind Ochino’s <note>Bernardino Ochino (1487–1564) was an influential Italian reformer whose spirited dialogues were widely read in the 16th and 17th centuries</note> apologues, however much he otherwise soars above the latter in shrewd, refined wit. A translator for our time thus finds himself in a predicament, the labor of which very few readers recognize or appreciate. He does not want to prune the beautiful floral and vine-like ornamentation <note>original: "Blumen- und Rankenwerk"; a metaphor for the dense, decorative, and often convoluted rhetorical flourishes characteristic of the Baroque era</note>, and yet he must, if Andreä is to become readable for us; and yet he must only do so to the extent that the beautiful, living plant not only loses nothing of its overall growth, but also stands before our eyes as if it had sprouted right in front of them. If a discerning eye and a light, skillful hand are not required for this, then I do not know what they should be for: for [taking] Andreä as he is, with

The immeasurable storehouse of his knowledge, the peculiar flexibility of his spirit toward all art and everything beautiful and worthy of knowing—and even more so the distracting busyness in which he lived, his early connections and social circles with the many types of people produced by the fermentation <note>original: "Gährung"; a reference to the intense social, religious, and intellectual unrest of the 17th century</note> of the previous century—none of this could lead him away from that "One True" thing, which is the constant spirit of his writings and which rises like a blossom out of every stylistic disguise <note>original: "Einkleidung"; referring to the allegorical or formal literary "clothing" Andreä used to present his ideas</note>.

<page-num>XI</page-num>

The reader who does not know Andreä will become acquainted with him through your historical introduction to his life; and if he desires more, he need only turn to the monument that has been set for him by the loyal hand of one of his patriotic countrymen in the *Wurttemberg Repertory* <note>original: "Wirtenbergischen Repertorium"; a reference to the *Wirtembergisches Repertorium der Litteratur*, a scholarly journal of the late 18th century dedicated to the history and literature of the Wurttemberg region</note> \*) . A man such as him must first be known through his life before one gets to know him through his writings: for writings in general—as much as they are a treacherous mirror for many—still only ever show us the surface of our heart and spirit.

But also as a writer of our fatherland, Andreä deserves the remembrance and love of his nation above so many who lived alongside him. Thoma-

But why, my dear sirs, should we believe this and not rather work against the modest forgetfulness of our fellow countrymen where it might harm them? Valentin Andreä belongs so truly to our own era that in many, many things I wish we had an Andreä now. Undeniably, the rays of the Enlightenment <term>Enlightenment (Aufklärung): the 18th-century intellectual movement that emphasized reason, science, and individual rights over traditional authority</term> have greatly increased over the last century; individual people in all social ranks think well and finely and rationally. However, the old scaffolding of prejudices, of abuses, and of corruptions in all affairs and social classes still stands in many lands and provinces of Germany just as it did in the days of the good Andreä

<sig>b 5</sig>

### Some Accounts regarding Andreä and the Editing of the Apologues.

**Before** Andreä is well-known to the readers, I cannot presume that they should observe him as attentively as this great, upright man deserves. This essay is therefore primarily intended for the readers of his Apologues <note>original: *Apologen*; these are short allegorical stories or fables intended to convey moral or social truths</note>. I consider him here only as a writer, regardless of the fact that I know he was far more than that. Authorship served him only as a means to his higher goals; it was not truly an end in itself for him. The period of this activity lasted until 1620, one year after the appearance of his Apologues, and my detailed accounts extend that far. The little information from then until his death, I wrote for those who perhaps still

<meta>This page continues the introduction to Johann Valentin Andreä’s biography, specifically identifying the primary sources used by the editor. It follows the previous page's mention of readers who might not yet be familiar with Andreä’s life and work.</meta>

do not know him at all. An excellent source from which I drew almost everything for this attempt at a history of his development as a writer was his handwritten autobiography <note>original: *Lebensbeschreibung*</note> left behind at his death, the use of which I owe to the accommodating kindness of the General Superintendent Herder <note>Johann Gottfried von Herder (1744–1803), a hugely influential German theologian, poet, and philosopher who admired Andreä</note>. While it is less rich regarding the actual history of his intellect than one might wish, it nonetheless contains many scattered details of importance. The first draft of it was prepared between 1614 and 1620; afterward, he seems to have continued it periodically, then by years, and finally by half-years. It reaches until very near his death. The actual purpose of its composition was a presentation of his shifting fortunes in civil life and the defense of his orthodoxy <term>Rechtgläubigkeit: the state of being "correct" in one's religious belief, particularly important for Andreä to prove his loyalty to Lutheranism amidst various controversies</term>.

Andreä* in the second volume of the prematurely interrupted *Wirtemberg Repertorium* <note>original: *Wirtembergisches Repertorium*; a scholarly journal of the 1780s</note>, pages 274–385, for which the handwritten life <note>original: *Vita manuscripta*</note>

Exactly two hundred years ago (on August 17, 1586), the man was born who, even without having enjoyed the education of our current age, stands as a human being and a scholar not far behind any of the most prominent great men of modern times.

His father, Johann Andreä—a son of Jacob Andreä, who is famous for the *History of the Formula of Concord* <note>The *Formula of Concord* (1577) was a definitive statement of Lutheran doctrine; Jacob Andreä was one of its primary authors and a dominant figure in 16th-century theology</note>—was at that time a preacher in Herrenberg in the territory of Württemberg and later died as an abbot at Königsbrunn. The first beneficial gift of Providence for his mind was bestowed upon Johann Valentin through the exceedingly delicate organization of his body. Consequently, the animal powers of man <note>A contemporary philosophical term referring to physical development, motor skills, and biological vitality as opposed to the "rational" or "spiritual" faculties</note> developed in him later than in ordinary children. For this reason, adults liked to have him around from an early age—specifically men of years and affairs <note>Referring to experienced men of business, administration, or high social standing</note>; from whose daily company the boy certainly received as much indirect benefit as they took direct pleasure in him.

In his fifth year, he moved with his father to Königsbrunn. He had already had a teacher in Herrenberg. "He was a good man" is all that the otherwise so grateful student knows to say in his praise. — Now he received, partly for public and partly for domestic instruction, six teachers in quick succession. He gained nothing from any of them, except insofar as each one was new. His mind was not brought as far by them as it was capable of going; however, the variety at least prevented him from coming to a complete standstill. <page-num>XXIX</page-num>

Stäudlin, whom Andreä’s parents raised as an orphan alongside their own children. He was two years younger than Valentin; possessing a very bright mind, he was always cheerful and gifted with excellent talents, though these ultimately did not advance him in the future simply because he tried to rise too quickly. His activity turned into restlessness, and his energies were worn away in vain.

A young physician, Johann Hartig, whom the Abbot <note>Referring to Andreä’s father, who served as the Lutheran Abbot of Königsbrunn</note> took on as both house physician and tutor, was Andreä’s first true teacher. With an amiable enthusiasm, Andreä confesses that he owes him almost everything. Although he does not describe Hartig’s method in detail, the way Andreä soon began to study independently makes it clear that it could not have been an ordinary one. He now began to occupy himself with extensive reading. Even then, his familiarity with Erasmus <note>Desiderius Erasmus (1466–1536), the famous Dutch humanist and scholar</note> and Frischlin <note>Nicodemus Frischlin (1547–1590), a German humanist, philologist, and satirical dramatist</note> took root, and their satirical writings certainly had all the impact of formal instruction for him; as likely as it is that merely the entertainment, which they

Around the same time (all before his twelfth year), he also read the history books of **Livy** <note>Titus Livius (64 BCE–17 CE), the famous Roman historian</note> and **Münster’s** <term>Geography</term> <note>Sebastian Münster’s *Cosmographia* (1544), the earliest German description of the world and a foundational text for geography</note>; and these became for the scholar what the former authors were for the future writer. Early historical reading makes a good mind more receptive to <term>polymathy</term> <note>The knowledge of many different subjects; universal learning</note> through the more lively play of ideas it provokes, and through the multitude of subjects it can only show in semi-darkness, it makes the mind more eager to seek them out. To this inclination for history—the foundations of which lay in Andreä’s natural talents and which were necessarily further developed through his engagement with Erasmus—geography naturally attached itself; and to geography, mathematics was linked by one of its ends. Even before the death of his father, which occurred in 1601, Andreä had lost his Hartig <note>Johann Hartig, his beloved tutor mentioned on the previous page</note>. Here he was so fortunate, alongside his brothers, to receive <term>tutors</term> <note>original: *Repetenten*; at the University of Tübingen, these were junior faculty members or graduate assistants who supervised and coached younger students in their lessons</note> whose knowledge and affection could only be surpassed by the zeal with which they devoted both entirely to him. One among them especially advanced his progress in mathematics. Having already been prepared by these studies, he grew accustomed—through his association with the son of a steel mill inspector named Fesel, his closest bosom friend—to paying closer attention to mechanical works. 

This interest, combined with his natural refined sense for beauty and encouraged by later chance circumstances, eventually led him to that extraordinary love for art and artworks in general, and especially for paintings. This passion was so uniquely his own among his contemporaries, and seemed such a true phenomenon for a **theologian** to possess, that they even mentioned it in his <term>epitaph</term> <note>original: *Grabschrift*; the inscription on a tombstone. It was considered unusual at the time for a churchman to be so deeply invested in secular art and aesthetics</note>. He now made himself

acquainted with artists of all kinds and very diligently visited their workshops.

In the second year of his stay in Tübingen, at sixteen years of age, he began to write. His first attempts were two Latin plays: *Esther* and *Hyacinthus*. The more distant reason why he emerged in this specific genre lies in his earlier reading; the more immediate cause was, as he says: the emulation of English actors <note>original: *anglicorum histrionum aemulatio*; this refers to the influential troupes of "English Comedians" who toured Germany in the early 17th century, bringing a more physical and professional style of theater</note>. Both plays, by the way, are lost. *Hyacinth*, however, still enjoyed the man's approval <note>Likely referring to a specific mentor or his former tutor mentioned on the previous page</note>. Results of his extensive reading and closer reflection upon much of what he read were: *Julius, or Concerning the State, in 3 Books* <note>original: *Julius, sive de politia l. 3.*</note> and *Astrological Judgment Against the Astrologers* <note>original: *Judicium astrologicum contra astrologos*</note>. Already at this time, his poetic works had a higher purpose than merely existing or simply entertaining. From then on, they served almost exclusively to teach more serious truths—not as a side note, but as their primary intent. Already now

"Perhaps Lipsius spoiled my style (a very well-founded concern! <note>Lipsius was often criticized for his "broken," abrupt Latin style, which departed from Ciceronian elegance</note>) but I had the advantage that he made me acquainted with antiquity, and taught me to value Seneca and Tacitus,"

<meta>This page continues the analysis of Johann Valentin Andreä's poetic nature, specifically explaining why, despite his clear talent and published works, he did not become a professional poet. It transitions from the previous page's mention of his poetic "attempts."</meta>

<page-num>XXXV</page-num>
<sig>c 2</sig>

attempts <note>original: *Versuche*; this completes the word from the previous page's catchword *Ver-*</note>, such as he provided in *Christian Painting* <note>original: *Christlich Gemäl*</note> and in *Spiritual Pastime* <note>original: *Geistlich Kurzweil*</note>, to say nothing of others. Why he, regardless of these, was not more a poet in the narrower sense of the word, the reason doubtless lay in his very particular situation. He belonged to that second class of poetic geniuses <term>Dichtergenies: individuals with inherent poetic talent who may or may not pursue it as a primary vocation</term> who have the natural disposition to be able to become poets, and commonly do become so, without being compelled to do so by that inner, irresistible urge which is the lot of only a very few. Through the choice of books for his first favorite readings, the interest of his spirit spread too early and too restlessly over the many various objects of the real world for him to be able, later on, to concentrate all his intellectual energy <term>Schnellkraft: literally "quick-power"; referring here to the spring-like elasticity or vital energy of the mind</term> upon the ideal world. Even then, that natural talent was already divided; and even if it were not, the active youth, from his eighteenth year onward, entered into too many demanding and distracting civil circumstances <term>bürgerliche Verhältnisse: the social, professional, and legal duties of a citizen's life</term> for leisure to have ever moved him to become a poet.

<summary>This page explains that while Andreä had significant poetic talent, his early intellectual interest in the real world and his subsequent professional duties prevented him from becoming a dedicated poet.</summary>

--- Page 42 ---
<meta>This page continues the explanation of why Johann Valentin Andreä did not pursue poetry as a primary vocation, transitioning from the discussion of his "second-rank" poetic talent and his preference for the real world over the ideal.</meta>

<page-num>XXXVI</page-num>

could. <note>original: *können*; this word completes the final sentence of the previous page: "...for leisure to have ever moved him to become a poet."</note> And a poetic genius of the second rank <term>Dichtergenie des zweiten Rangs: a term used here to describe someone with great talent who requires an external catalyst to create, rather than being driven by an uncontrollable inner obsession</term> must be prompted if it is to dedicate itself exclusively to the art of poetry. More appropriate to the direction his spirit had already taken, more compatible with the duties of an active life, and more interesting for his already manifesting desire to see the world, was the tireless study of historians and geographers, especially contemporary ones.

He names among them: Bembo, Giovio, Guicciardini, Sigonius; Ortelius, Theodor de Bry, Mercator, Merula, and Thuanus. <note>These represent the leading lights of Renaissance scholarship: Pietro Bembo, Paolo Giovio, Francesco Guicciardini, Carlo Sigonio (historians); Abraham Ortelius, Theodor de Bry, Gerardus Mercator, Paulus Merula (geographers/cartographers); and Jacques Auguste de Thou (historian).</note> He loved the latter <note>Thuanus</note> so much that even in later years he undertook an abridgment of his history. He applied exceptional diligence to Zwinger's *Theater of Human Life* <note>original: *Theatrum humanae vitae*; a massive, influential 16th-century encyclopedia of human knowledge and behavior by Theodor Zwinger</note>, which provided equal nourishment for his inclination toward anthropology <term>Menschenkunde: literally "knowledge of man"; the study of human nature, character, and social behavior</term> and history. Perhaps this book stands in a closer connection with his *Christian Mythology* <note>original: *Mythologia christiana*; one of Andreä's later works that uses allegory to critique society</note>. He also perfected his mathematical knowledge more and more, supported by books, instruments, and instruction from

His amusements were literary in nature, or at least they remained restricted to the circle of a quiet domestic life. For six years, Andreä drank no wine except when at his mother’s house—and one must remember the social atmosphere of that drunken era. <note>Andreä lived during a period in the late 16th and early 17th centuries when heavy drinking was a common social expectation, particularly among university students.</note> Even before his twentieth year, he was already earning 100 Florins <term>Florins (Fl.): The "Gulden" or gold coin used in the Holy Roman Empire; 100 Florins was a significant annual sum for a young scholar.</term> annually, which he used to support his mother. Following the death of his father, she had already entrusted the management of the household to him, even though he was only fifteen years old at the time and had older siblings. Moreover, he was generally loved and highly esteemed throughout the city of Tübingen. His true ambition was to become a preacher. <page-num>XLIV</page-num>

[to dedicate] himself, Andreä returned to his homeland. He paid his respects to the Duke <note>Duke Johann Friedrich of Württemberg (1582–1628), who reigned during a period of significant religious and political tension.</note>. The Duke also expressed a serious desire to be able to employ him in state affairs, but even now Andreä declined this; he remained a theologian. This persistence is truly remarkable. It certainly would not have been difficult for a young, fiery man with such extensive knowledge to transition into <term>Staatswissenschaften: The "sciences of the state," encompassing political science, law, and administration.</term>, and the path of his activities in that field would have been far less hindered than in the church. The theology of his time possessed exactly everything that should have distanced his spirit and heart from it; its speculations were extremely barren in terms of valuable results, yet its fiery zeal for "literary bonfires" <note>A metaphor for the aggressive censorship, book burnings, and character assassinations common during the era of Lutheran Orthodoxy.</note> for those who thought differently was all the more abundant. From Lauingen he had applied for a preaching office and received the reply that he could never hope for a position of that kind in the territory of Württemberg. Despite all of this, he still chose the clerical profession as the point from which he wanted to work for the welfare of humanity.

Phoebus is another name for Apollo, the Greek god of light and poetry.</note> <note>I left this pithy piece as I found it, in order to provide a kind of *Esprit d'Andreae* <gloss>French: the wit or spirit of Andreä</gloss>.</note>, *Truth in Exile*, and the *Metamorphosis*; they would be too foreign for us, and might also be questionable for modern tastes. ->XLIX<-

towards modest doubters and those who think differently. He scourges hypocrisy without mercy, exposing it in all its shameful nakedness to public abhorrence. With astonishing courage, he rebukes the despotism of princes, the false statecraft of their servants, and the scheming of courtiers. The pedantry of men of letters, their shallow word-learning, and his earnest plea to treat the sciences more worthily, are a central theme. He ridicules alchemy, astrology, mysticism, and <term>syllable-splitting: A pedantic obsession with minor linguistic details or trivialities.</term>; he warns against the abuse of <term>polymathy: Having broad but often superficial knowledge across many subjects.</term> and poetry, and recommends with all the warmth of a close friend a deeper study of the Hebrew and Greek languages, mathematics, economics, and physics—indeed, more practical methods in education. Favorite ideas, which he presents again and again in new attire, include: <term>theodicy: The defense of God's goodness and providence despite the existence of suffering and evil.</term> concerning the adverse fate of individuals; the vindication of noble souls who failed in great undertakings; and a patriotic spirit, most audible in the exhortations to Ger- <note>original: *deut-*. This is an incomplete word, likely *deutschen* (German).</note>

man nature and to remain true to truth; the entry of human beings into civil society, the deviations according to various circumstances, misery, and the return to happiness; humility and devotion to God, or, if one prefers, self-denial and resignation in moral, literary, and political matters, for the good of the whole and for one’s own future perfection. In terms of form, one could call his <term>apologues: Short stories or fables, often featuring personified animals or objects, intended to provide a moral lesson.</term> <note>original: *Apologos*</note> "historical paramythia." <note>original: *Paramythien*. A term, later popularized by thinkers like Herder, referring to fables or poetic tales that provide moral "counsel" or encouragement by reimagining traditional myths or history.</note> For the most part, his characters are taken from actual history; what he has them do is adapted to their character, often based in part on a real fact, only modified for current needs. The composition of the pieces is too unique to allow for their classification into a known category; and yet they are so varied that I did not dare to create a new one, because it would have been very difficult to establish a theory. critics feared) <note>Completing the sentence from the previous page: "...the arbitrary theories of many art critics feared."</note> lies right in the middle between allegory, fable, and epigram, so that its territory overlaps with all three. Some are true fables, many are prosaic epigrams. The tone of the narrative has a touch of simple **good-naturedness** <note>original: *Bonhommie*</note>. However, since this tone often loses itself in whimsy and wit, and what is said in that guise might now seem too commonplace, I was not always anxious to maintain it.

The Mythology is the essence of all his contemplations on the affairs of humanity. Many of these he later elaborated further in specific writings. I have sought to present the spirit of the Mythology, and in doing so, have also partially presented the spirit of his most important writings. Of the strictly scientific works, there are only a few. The rest naturally fall into two categories: they are offerings of friendship and gratitude, and gifts to humanity. Perhaps no man ever wrote so much for his friends—**apologies** <note>original: *Apologien*. In this historical context, an "apology" is not an admission of fault, but a formal written defense of a person, idea, or belief.</note>

->d 2<-

<summary>This page analyzes the literary style of Johann Valentin Andreä, describing his work as a blend of allegory, fable, and epigram dedicated to the betterment of humanity and the service of his friends.</summary>

<keywords>literary criticism, allegory, fables, epigrams, Johann Valentin Andreä, humanitarianism, apologies, literary genre</keywords>

during their lifetimes and monuments of honor after death. <note>Completing the thought from the previous page regarding Andreä's written defenses and tributes for his companions.</note> But I also know of no person in history for whom friendship in general was more of a necessity. His autobiography <note>original: *Lebensbeschreibung*</note> proves—perhaps nothing so strongly as this. He makes it his duty to recount for us, in every one of his relationships, the names of the people he loved, or with whom he at least associated on friendly terms; and the man was never more interesting to me than in those lists of names that seem so tedious. Even when he wrote nothing else, he still wrote about his friends. ---

The purpose of these works was, as already mentioned, to most urgently recommend the improvement of education in both moral and literary matters, genuine practical Christianity within a strict system of ethics <note>original: *Sittenlehre*</note>, charity in particular, and a withdrawal from sensuality, as well as freedom of spirit <note>original: *Geistesfreiheit*</note> and an aversion to the fanatical <note>original: *schwärmerischen*</note> secret societies of his age. To be sure, he does this very often directly—

<meta>This page continues the analysis of Johann Valentin Andreä’s literary style and the reception of his work, following the list of his publications from 1619–1620 on the previous page. LV
d 4

and with captivating eloquence; on the whole, however, he most frequently and successfully made use of a laughing mood and bitter mockery <note>original: *Spott*</note> directed at the opposing follies. He is the strongest proof that mockery and enthusiasm can indeed unite for the benefit of humanity. He mocked; and yet he acted with more warmth than those who so decisively deny it—those who merely declaim. But this very tone—likely combined with the martial unrest of that period, when one province could not influence another because Germany was still a stranger to itself, and its common sons only came to know each other through atrocities <note>A reference to the Thirty Years' War (1618–1648), which devastated Germany and isolated its various territories through extreme violence.</note>—was the reason for the narrower sphere of influence of this restless writer. He did have influence; his writings caused a sensation and brought about good; they helped form the worthy educator Amos Comenius <note>John Amos Comenius (1592–1670), a pioneering Czech educator and theologian who was deeply inspired by Andreä's ideas on social and educational reform.</note>, yet they still had too little effect relative to their inner power. And that was due, I believe, to their style <note>original: *Manier*</note>. A public that is led by philanthropic mockery

LVI

to be [influenced in this way], must already stand at a high level of cultivation. And in this regard, Andreä’s age was receptive only to deafening fanaticism <note>original: *Schwärmereien*</note> on the one hand, and coarse pranks on the other.

And this style <term>Manier: The specific artistic or literary 'manner' or mode of expression characteristic of an author.</term> peculiar to Andreä, which presents the teaching of truth so vividly, provided the inspiration for a reader <note>original: *Lesebuch*; a collection of curated texts for educational or general reading.</note> for our own age. My choice fell upon pieces that seemed appropriate to me, and which I hoped I could make enjoyable for the reader; for often, I could not provide his flawed phrasing <note>literally: 'clothing' or 'attire' (Einkleidung)</note> with any sense of propriety through mere re-wording. Yet these fragments contain the most excellent examples of the genre of apologues <term>Apologue: A moral fable, often featuring animals or personified objects, intended to convey a useful lesson.</term>. I am therefore providing no literal translation (in which my author could hardly show himself to advantage), but rather Andreä’s thoughts in Andreä’s style, with modifications to what was offensive or incomprehensible. I have sought to remedy these issues through reworking and by making the historical allusions more immediate to the present day. With what success this has been achieved, I must leave to the judgment of a fair-minded <note>original: *billigen*—in this context, it means equitable or impartial rather than inexpensive.</note> public,

LVII
d 5

from them I await the decision whether I may hope for a lenient reception — the kindest I can expect.

An investigation into whether and to what extent he was serious about Rosicrucianism <term>Rosencreutzerei: The Rosicrucian movement, a secret society of mystics and alchemists that claimed to possess ancient wisdom. It was a subject of intense debate in the 17th century.</term> does not belong to my plan. And even then, I would have sought to avoid it. The dispute over this has been conducted between judges who are too competent for me to dare to take a side; especially since I am too little acquainted with the matter itself. In my current conviction, however, he is completely innocent. My reasons are: <term>Andreä</term> <note>Johann Valentin Andreä (1586–1654), the German theologian and satirist who is the subject of this work.</note> wrote the *Chemical Wedding* <term>Chymische Hochzeit: *The Chemical Wedding of Christian Rosenkreutz* (1616), one of the foundational, yet highly allegorical and satirical, manifestos of Rosicrucianism.</term> in his fifteenth year; his travels <term>Ordensreisen: Travels undertaken for the purpose of a secret order or society.</term> were too fleeting to be those of an Order's mission; he solemnly renounced the *Fama* <term>Fama: Referring to the *Fama Fraternitatis*, the first Rosicrucian manifesto, which Andreä later distanced himself from, calling it a joke or "ludibrium."</term>; furthermore, in his biography <term>Biographie: Referring to Andreä’s autobiography, *Vita ab ipso conscripta*, where he reflects on his life and works.</term>, one finds no trace of secret intentions. Therefore, whoever believes the opposite will forgive me if, for the sake of Andreä’s noble and free character, I do not yet join their opinion.

Amidst restless endeavors to do something for the happiness of his brothers, the venerable man had now spent six years in Vaihin- <note>The text ends with the fragment "Vachin-", which is a contemporary spelling for the town of Vaihingen, where Andreä served as a church superintendent.</note>

LVIII

...lived in Vaihingen. <note>The word fragment "chingen" completes "Vaihingen," where Andreä served as a deacon before his promotion.</note> In his thirty-fourth year, in 1620, he became Superintendent <note>A high-ranking administrative and spiritual leader in the Lutheran church, roughly equivalent to a bishop in other denominations.</note> in Calw. Now the brilliance of the man of action overshadows the writer. Here is my goal, and I lay down my pen; out of reverence—from this point onward, I would be even less able than before to depict him in a manner worthy of him—but saddened by my own sense of weakness despite such great zeal and the desire to let others feel what I felt as I accompanied him in his deeds and sufferings until his grave, and felt my own human dignity so deeply. Perhaps some readers do not know him at all; for them, let these few individual traits be shared.

In Calw, he remained for nineteen years. Here he founded a <term>Christian Society: originally *Societas Christiana*, a fraternal organization intended to reform society through practical Christian ethics and mutual support.</term> which bears the name of the Dyers’ Foundation <note>original: *Färbergestift*. Calw was a major center for the cloth and dyeing industry.</note>. Through the charitable contributions of the citizens, a capital fund was collected; from its interest, industrious craftsmen were supported, the poor, the sick, and the infirm were cared for, needy students were assisted, and a city library was established. Through prudence and restless zeal, he increased—

LIX

[he increased] it so much that, despite the terrible general inflation and scarcity <note>original: *Theurung*. This refers to the extreme economic distress and food shortages that plagued Germany during the war.</note> which the Thirty Years' War <note>A series of devastating conflicts in Central Europe between 1618 and 1648, which caused massive loss of life and social collapse.</note> soon brought about, he was nevertheless able to help not only the poor of his city, but even suffering strangers! A great number of children were fed, taught, and apprenticed to craftsmen at the society’s expense. Within five years, over a million people <note>This likely refers to the total number of individual acts of aid or meals provided, rather than a million unique individuals, given the population of the time.</note> owed their alms and care to this foundation. Even the preachers and schoolteachers driven out by the imperial soldiers <note>The Catholic forces of the Holy Roman Empire, who displaced Protestant clergy during their military campaigns.</note> in 1630 enjoyed the fruits of his philanthropy. 

Finally, Calw was also devastated; and Andreä himself was forced to suffer want. His house had gone up in flames; and with it, all his manuscripts, paintings, and other works of art, the contemplation of which had been his only recreation until then. The correspondence with his friends ceased entirely; among his fellow citizens, the most terrible ruin and disorder prevailed; the poor languished from hunger and disease; swarms of orphans wailed naked in the streets for food; and vile

LX

Andreä himself, who had so often become a burden to them as a strict moral teacher <term>Sittenlehrer: a teacher of ethics and social conduct</term> and active Christian, was oppressed by them. And yet he took the remnants of his fortune, gathered contributions under incredible difficulties, and provided for eighty orphans. In other matters, too, he demonstrated unshakable courage. Hardly had he taken office when he took up the cause of several innocently oppressed citizens; without regard for the cabal <note>original: *Cabale*. Referring to a secret plot or a group of political schemers.</note> of their powerful opponents, he prevailed upon the Prince <term>Fürst: the sovereign ruler of the territory</term> on their behalf. Even with the enemy soldiers, he often eased the city's fate through his intercessions. It is even said that, in order to be able to hearten his congregation in the event of a sudden attack, he sometimes preached with a sword under his cloak. He always championed the youth most emphatically; right from the start, he greatly improved many aspects of the method of public instruction; and was such a friend to children <term>Kinderfreund: a term for a dedicated educator or benefactor of children</term> that the children, toward this grave and strict man,

LXI

hopped toward him in the street and kissed his hands.

In the year 1639, the Duke summoned him to Stuttgart as a court preacher <term>Hofprediger: a senior clergyman appointed to serve the spiritual needs of a royal or noble court</term>. He resisted quite earnestly, but the Prince and his friends compelled him to go. What likely encouraged him the most was the prospect of finding a wider sphere of influence there for his plans to improve many important matters. The Prince was weak, his deputies were harsh and arbitrary, and Andreä's assistants were lazy or envious. From every side, obstacles were placed in his path; and yet, he accomplished a great deal. The theological seminary <term>Stift: an endowed educational institution, specifically the Tübinger Stift, which trained clergy</term>, a fertile nursery for worthy men, had completely collapsed. By 1641, through his foresight, it once again supported fifty pupils. Gradually, he gave it an entirely new form, appointed two new teachers for mathematics and the Hebrew language, and paid their salaries himself through collected contributions. He also restored the gymnasium <term>Gymnasium: a high-level secondary school focused on classical learning and university preparation</term> in Stuttgart, replacing the teachers and their salaries. The

LXII

Church servants <note>original: "Kirchendiener," referring here to the lower-ranking clergy and ministers</note> dishonored the dignity of their station through low behavior; he alleviated their poverty, but in return made all the stricter, yet no more than just, demands upon their conduct. Simony <term>Simony: the buying or selling of ecclesiastical privileges or offices, a practice Andreä deeply despised</term> raged everywhere at that time; he was indeed unable to suppress it, for it originated from the court itself, but he nevertheless worked against it with all his might. At the same time, the burden of his office lay doubly upon him. His colleague was either ill or away at court; he had to work for two, and yet his salary over three years did not exceed 40 guilders <term>Gülden: a currency used in the German states; forty guilders over three years was a pittance, especially for a high-ranking court preacher</term>. The Treasury <note>original: "Cammer," the administrative body managing the state's finances</note> had to restrict itself in this regard so that it could afford the wages of hunters and stable masters, and the gifts bestowed upon jugglers and entertainers. His friends abroad, especially Duke August of Brunswick, supported him. Thus, his house could still remain a refuge for those in need; and he was still able to raise several of the children of his relatives and friends. And yet, his love for his fatherland remained too strong for him to have left it, as urgently as his

LXIII

Nuremberg friends and Duke August invited him to come to them. He wished to remain in the territory of Württemberg <note>original: "Wirtembergischen"</note>, but sought at least to be released from his service. As little as he was supported, his public utility <note>his dedication to the common good and social reform</note> was nevertheless recognized, and they were harsh enough to deny him even that request. Finally, however, in 1650, he was transferred to Bebenhausen <note>a prominent former Cistercian monastery near Tübingen</note> as Abbot; and just a few weeks before his death, he was appointed Abbot of Adelberg. 

But everything now appeared to him in a dark, mournful shadow; his cheerfulness had become somber despondency, and he lived on only to feel the painful weight of having lived. His zeal for humanity still possessed youthful strength, but his physical powers were those of an old man. Friendship was such an essential necessity for his happiness, yet all his more intimate confidants were dying around him; he remained the last one left, and around him were nothing but strangers. His courage was gone; the persecutions of his adversaries <note>original: "Widerwärtigen," referring to those who opposed his ecclesiastical reforms and his Rosicrucian-inspired social ideals</note> now bowed him low, and he felt every slan-

LXIV

—der, especially that directed at his orthodoxy <note>original: "Rechtgläubigkeit"; his adherence to established church doctrine was often questioned by conservative peers due to his reformist ideas</note>, which had been so violently attacked from the very beginning. All of this made him feel his completely shattered health twofold. During his final years, he enjoyed only a few bearable weeks. How forgivable, how human then were his bitter complaints and his heartfelt wish to pass into a better life. The last solace of his days remained his friendship with the noble August <note>Duke August the Younger of Brunswick-Wolfenbüttel (1579–1666), a celebrated scholar and book collector</note>, which he maintained through a most diligent correspondence. His most ardent wish was to see him one last time. He was ready for the journey when a violent illness cast him onto his deathbed. On June 27, 1654, he dictated one last letter to his friend; he then intended to sign it. He wrote two letters—and passed away.

<meta>This page begins a new section titled "The Masks." The previous page concluded the biography of Johann Valentin Andreä on page LXIV. This page introduces a satirical allegory about the state of scholarship.</meta>

---

# The Masks.

---

-> <-

Recently, a rumor circulated that Augustine, Papinian, Hippocrates, Aristotle, Euclid, Tacitus, Cicero, Virgil, and several other leading figures of the Republic of Letters <note>original: "Gelehrtenrepublik"; the international community of scholars and intellectuals who communicated across borders through Latin</note>, were on their way back from a banquet when they encountered a troop of mischievous fellows called satirists and were mistreated by them. The matter caused quite a stir, and it was generally agreed that it deserved the strictest punishment. Yet, as tragic as it began, the outcome was equally comical. For certain Harlequins <note>stock characters from Italian theater known for their masks and buffoonery</note> had assumed the clothing and masks of those famous men

<summary>A satirical narrative begins describing a supposed assault on the "greats" of history (philosophers, poets, and scientists) by satirists, only to reveal that the victims were actually imposters in masks.</summary>
<keywords>The Masks, Republic of Letters, satire, allegory, Harlequins, intellectual history</keywords>

--- Page 72 ---
<meta>This page continues the narrative from page 1, where pranksters (Harlequins) have disguised themselves as famous historical scholars and thinkers to cause mischief in the streets.</meta>

...assumed, paraded through the streets with pitch torches, and committed all the follies of drunkards. Whatever the intent of the farce might have been—whether to play a joke on the residents or to defame those worthy elders—the ending was not the most pleasant for the performers. Some patriots <note>civic-minded citizens who cared for the town's order</note>, who found it strange to encounter such distinguished men as revelers <note>original: "Nachtschwärmer"; literally "night-moths," a term for people who wander the streets late at night carousing</note> here, investigated more closely, and the deception was discovered. It came to blows, and with the help of several others, they put an end to the masquerade. 

This incident soon reached the ears of the common folk <note>original: "Pöbel"; referring to the uneducated masses or the "rabble"</note>; but, as usual, they only heard half the story, which led to various bitter remarks about the actual historical figures themselves. It was uncommonly difficult to teach the people better, until finally those "fathers of the city" <note>the actual scholars or those representing their intellectual legacy</note> publicly swore that no harm had come to them, and on the contrary, that the satirists were their closest friends.

### Hypochondria

---

Once Curiosity encountered Xanthus, who crept about more like a skeleton <note>Original: *Knochenmann*; literally "bone-man," a common German personification for Death or an extremely emaciated person.</note> than a man, and rattled through the streets with his withered skin. Without knowing the philosopher, she was moved by his wretched appearance.

"My friend! What did you do," she asked him, "to be shackled to the cart?" <note>An idiom referring to being forced into hard labor or punishment, like a convict pulling a heavy load.</note> — "Who do you take me for?" replied Xanthus, wrinkling his brow up and down.

"Hmm! For a man condemned to the galley or—to the school lectern." <note>The "Schulkatheder" refers to the teacher’s podium, suggesting that teaching was viewed as a form of exhausting, soul-crushing labor equivalent to being a galley slave.</note> — "I am neither!"

### How Things Tend to Go.

"O dear friend!" said a mute man to a blind man, "if you should see a skilled harpist, do me the favor and point him out to me: my deaf son would dearly like some entertainment <note>original: *Zeitvertreib*; literally a 'pastime' or 'way to pass the time'</note>." "Just now," was the blind man's reply, "I saw a virtuoso of that kind; if you wish to have him sought out, my lame messenger <note>original: *Läufer*; literally a 'runner,' referring to a servant kept by noble households to deliver messages on foot</note> is at your service." The messenger received his orders, and as he galloped through street after street, he stumbled upon a harpist without arms. A bargain was struck, and the musician <term>Tonkünstler: literally 'tone-artist,' a term used in the 18th century to elevate the status of musicians as practitioners of a fine art</term> was hired for the deaf man’s ensemble <note>original: *Capelle*; referring to a private musical group or orchestra</note>. He paid his compliments to the waiting company and played—without playing—so beautifully that the deaf man was all ears with delight, and even the blind man found the man’s appearance to be beautiful,

### The Oath of Office and Social Station.

---

With extraordinary solemnity and a very venerable wrinkled brow, Routine <note>original: *Schlendrian*; a personification of the slow, habitual, and unthinking way of doing things, often associated with bureaucratic "red tape" or outdated traditions</note> had the oath of allegiance sworn to him. Accordingly, the servants of the sanctuary <note>referring to the clergy</note> swore an oath of pure doctrine and exemplary conduct; the guardians of justice [swore to] impartial judgments and an abhorrence of bribes <note>original: *Geschenken*; literally "gifts," but in this legal context, bribes</note>; the guardians of the state [swore] zeal for liberty and hatred of luxury; the students of wisdom [swore] tireless research in the treasure-troves of human knowledge; the historians [swore] respect for the future testimony of contemporary youth and public utility for posterity; the statesmen [swore the] selection of the noblest means to achieve the noblest intentions; the working class [swore] usefulness for common life and indifference toward

### Doctors and Masters.

---

The State recently encountered the Academy. Even from a distance, his displeased expression and quickened pace led her to expect reproaches. And indeed, his address was not the most pleasant. "Three 'splendid' scholars you have sent me again recently! By heaven! Those are the ones to whom I can entrust my affairs!" <note>The State is speaking sarcastically here, frustrated by the lack of practical skill in the graduates the university has produced.</note>

Have <term>Prometheus</term><note>In Greek mythology, Prometheus was the titan who fashioned the first humans out of clay; the Academy is suggesting the State should seek a divine creator if it wants perfect servants.</note> carve your servants for you!"

### Doctors and Masters.

The State recently encountered the Academy. Even from a distance, his displeased expression and quickened pace signaled that she should expect reproaches. Indeed, his greeting was not the most pleasant. "Three 'splendid' scholars you have sent me again recently! By heaven! To them I can surely entrust my affairs!" <note>The State is speaking sarcastically here, frustrated with the lack of practical skill in the graduates.</note>

The Academy fell into embarrassment. Shame and resentment prompted her to reply: "Well! Then let Prometheus <note>In Greek mythology, the Titan Prometheus fashioned the first humans out of clay; the Academy suggests the State is asking for impossible, custom-made perfection.</note> fashion your servants for you!"

An answer that was bound to provoke the State even more! — "Shall I describe your polymaths <note>original: "Polyhistors"; a term for scholars with universal knowledge across many fields.</note> to you? ### The Dowry.

---

Marriage <note>original: *Ehstand*; personified here as a male figure</note> had to listen to the bitterest reproaches from all sides. imperious housekeeper <note>original: *Wirthschafterin*; referring to the Dowry personified in the previous page</note> her dismissal, and hired **Piety**, an amiable girl from the family of **Virtue**. But his hope to remedy the complaints through this change failed. In vain! Even though the new housekeeper treated everyone and everything with kindness and consideration, and managed the household in an exemplary manner! Scarcely had her predecessor—that coquettish servant of **Pleasure**—been missed; scarcely had people begun to suspect that **Labor** and **Moderation** <note>original: *Mässigkeit*; also implies temperance or frugality</note> should again become the norm—immediately there arose such a loud and persistent clamor and outcry from the discontented, that is to say, from the entire public, that the **Dowry** had to be restored to her previous position. Since then, she has become all the more arrogant and acts as a despot within the domestic economy of marriage <note>original: *Oekonomie des Ehstandes*</note>, which she has managed to bring entirely under her control, even to the point of tyranny.

<summary>In this satirical allegory, Marriage attempts to replace the Dowry with Piety and Virtue, but public outcry forces him to reinstate the Dowry. The text concludes that financial interests now hold tyrannical sway over the institution of marriage.</summary>
<keywords>allegory, marriage, Dowry, Piety, Virtue, social satire, 18th-century domestic life, domestic economy</keywords>

### Nothing.

---

The city of **Lavernia** <note>Lavernia is likely a satirical reference to Laverna, the Roman goddess of thieves, cheats, and hypocrites</note> is so famous for its **manufactories** <term>Manufacturen; early industrial workshops or factories</term> that many people are always seeking work there. Among others, a strong young man arrived and placed an advertisement in the newspaper: An **artist** <term>Künstler; here used in the sense of a "virtuoso" or one possessing a unique, often performative skill</term> who could make *Nothing* out of *Something* wished to be employed at a factory. To everyone, it seemed quite ridiculous to recommend oneself for an art that had already become commonplace through the habits of **alchemists** <note>who were often viewed as frauds who turned gold into nothing through failed experiments</note> and **bon vivants** <note>original: *Bonvivants*; literally "good livers," referring to people who spend their wealth excessively on pleasure until nothing remains</note>. Only the **statesmen**, with their deep-penetrating discernment, deemed him worthy of attention. They summoned him to a secret interview and, after repeated promises on his part, presented him with the following questions:

"Do you dare <note>The statesmen address the man using the formal third-person "Er," a historical German style used by superiors when speaking to subordinates</note> to make *nothing* out of the great **grievances of the people**? To make nothing

<summary>A new satirical narrative begins in the city of Lavernia, where an "artist" claims he can turn "something into nothing." While the public finds the claim absurd, the local politicians realize this skill is perfect for making public complaints disappear.</summary>

To make nothing out of the great **perks** <term>Sporteln; supplementary fees or "tips" traditionally collected by officials, often synonymous with petty corruption</term> that we take? To make nothing out of great **excesses**? To make nothing out of **Hell** and the **Last Judgment**? <note>The statesmen are asking if the artist can make the public forget their financial burdens and the elites forget their moral consequences.</note>

To all of this, he offered his services with great confidence and even presented plans for it. He was now showered with honors and gifts. With a triumphant, mocking smile, he now spoke to those who had previously ridiculed him:

"To make *something* out of *nothing*—any conscientious fool can do that; but to make *nothing* out of *something*—this fortunate invention was reserved for our age!"

### The Ghost.

---

Recently a ghost made its appearance. Its head was wrapped in black and white bandages; it had fiery hands and horse's hooves; and the rest of its body was covered with various toys along with symbolic paintings. It carried reversed weapons <note>In traditional military and state funerals, weapons were carried reversed—pointed backward or downward—as a sign of mourning and submission to death.</note> and wept as if it wanted to laugh. Exorcists were called for. They struggled for a long time in vain to find its name; finally, it named itself. “Funeral Procession <note>original: "Leichenbegängnifs"</note> is my name!” But just then, Death intervened. He refused to recognize it as his brother. “Look upon me,” he spoke indignantly, “a simple being, and upon you, a mere mass of clothing, and judge for yourself!” The ghost summoned the pastor and the sexton <term>Küster; a parish official responsible for the church building and assisting in services, including funerals</term> to testify to its spiritual nature; and as the most valid proof of this, it passed the funeral sermons <term>Leichenpredigten; a popular early modern genre of printed sermons that praised the life of the deceased, often with great exaggeration</term>, in which it was mentioned most laudably, among the spectators...

<summary>This page presents a satirical allegory where a "Funeral Procession" is personified as a ridiculous ghost. It highlights the conflict between the stark reality of Death and the elaborate, materialistic vanity of mourning rituals and eulogies.</summary>
<keywords>satire, allegory, Death, funeral rites, mourning customs, funeral sermons, religious critique, personification</keywords>

--- Page 110 ---
<meta>This page continues the allegorical fable "The Ghost" from page 33. The personified "Funeral Procession" has been trying to prove its spiritual importance to Death and a group of onlookers by presenting exaggerated funeral sermons.</meta>

...to go about. Unfortunately, it was asked whether it had been present at the burial of the founder of our religion <note>A reference to Jesus Christ, whose burial was famously simple compared to the "pomp" being critiqued here.</note> and his first disciples and his martyrs <note>original: "Blutzeugen," literally "blood-witnesses," a traditional term for those killed for their faith.</note>. At that, it fell silent. "If you can one day on the great Day of Judgment" — a frank Christian now settled the matter — "confidently approach the Judge with all this pomp, then you shall be honored as a brother of a Christian death; until then, you must endure being pitied as the most foolish creature of foolish human vanity."

### Alchemy

The rival of the creative Deity, that art which lends wings to the slow steps of nature toward perfection, and—equal in vital force to the sun—elevates mercury, through an ever-ascending scale, into gold, had now exhausted her rich coffers; and contempt and hunger now weighed upon her. The meanest mocked her; she was reviled by the mischievous, and the judgment of the more severe even assigned punishments to her. She was further struck by the suspicion of counterfeiting <note>original: "Münzverfälschung," the illegal debasement or forging of currency</note> and every kind of fraud practiced by bankrupts <note>original: "Bankeroteurs," a term specifically for those who fraudulently declared bankruptcy to hide assets.</note>. People asked her why her "fortunate powder" <note>likely a reference to the "Powder of Projection," a form of the Philosopher's Stone believed to transmute base metals into gold</note>, the result of such restless striving and so many experiments, nourished her only in the most meager way? Sighs and laments over the obscurity of her law-books, over the fragility of her tools, over unfavorable constellations <note>astrological alignments once thought essential for the success of alchemical "work"</note>, and the unfortunate mixing of the

<meta>This page continues the personified speech of Alchemy. Earths—were her answer and excuse. Hope and the inner delight of an inquiring spirit, she continued, compensate me for the indignity suffered from the mob. So much fruitless expense?—it does not grieve me. Without sacrifice, no mortal penetrates the Holy of Holies <note>original: "Allerheiligste," a reference to the innermost sanctuary of a temple, here used metaphorically for the ultimate secrets of nature.</note> of nature. What pains me is that with the last penny <note>original: "Groschen," a small silver coin; its loss signifies total financial ruin.</note>, the last little spark of the holy fire <note>In alchemy, the "holy fire" refers both to the literal constant heat required for the Great Work and the spiritual passion of the practitioner.</note> was also gone.

And yet, enough always remains to me. I invented medicines; that preserves my honor. I can go begging;—a remedy against hunger! And my talent for making promises <note>original: "Verheissungen," referring to the grandiose alchemical claims of transmuting base metals or achieving immortality.</note> will always bring me some students from among the more simple-minded of the people.

### The Incorrigibles.

---

At the command of Virtue, the wise were required to craft various instruments to rectify the distortions in the morality of the human race. Indeed, many efforts devoted to this cause met with success. What time and habit had made crooked could, for the most part, be straightened again. Thus, for example, the hotheads, the "know-it-alls," the spendthrifts, the chatterboxes, the flighty, and others whose morality <note>original: "Sittlichkeit," referring here to social and ethical conduct</note> had taken a wrong turn through the recklessness of youth, were successfully reformed through the lessons of experience. 

But two categories of people render every new attempt at reform perpetually fruitless. Whether it be that the alchemist's <note>original: "Goldmacher," literally "gold-maker," used here as a term of derision for those seeking the philosopher's stone</note> intellect has wasted away <note>original: "verlaborirt," a clever pun suggesting the squandering of life and resources specifically through laboratory work</note> both money and health, he remains an eternal slave to

<summary>This page presents an allegory where Virtue attempts to "straighten" human character flaws, succeeding with most vices but failing with two "incorrigible" types, the first being the obsessed alchemist.</summary>
<keywords>Virtue, morality, reform, alchemy, vice, social satire, education</keywords>

Hope. And the vanity of the fair sex <note>original: "ſchöne Geſchlecht," a standard 18th and 19th-century idiom for women</note>—let all the mockery of the wise, all the despotism of princes, and all the threats of want <note>original: "Mangels," referring here to poverty or the lack of financial means</note> conspire against it; it will always be protected by the spirit of pettiness and elevated by the charm of novelty! It leans to this side, only to snap to the opposite in an instant. We would sooner discover the squaring of the circle <note>An ancient mathematical puzzle proved impossible to solve, used here to represent a hopeless endeavor</note> than a dress code <note>original: "Kleiderordnung," referring to sumptuary laws which historically attempted to restrict what people could wear based on their social rank</note> for the fair sex.

->The Latinist.<-

---

A schoolmaster <note>original: "Schulmann," referring to a professional academic or teacher, often used satirically in this period to denote someone pedantic</note> had encountered the Greek and Hebrew languages, and, following the custom of monarchs, had passed by without tipping his hat. For a long time now, they had not been well-disposed toward this creature; they therefore seized this opportunity to express it. They caught up with him, and a sound thrashing took the place of all useless reproaches. Let them not be accused, therefore, of any lack of breeding <note>original: "Lebensart," a term for social etiquette and refined manners</note>. There are people whose constitution renders every other medicine ineffective, the <term>panacea</term> <note>A legendary universal remedy; here the author uses it ironically to refer to corporal punishment</note> of the stick excepted. The pedagogue made a great clamor and called upon heaven and hell for help. People did indeed rush to the scene, but they could not lift a finger for laughing. At last, the Latin language heard how its apprentice was faring and came to his aid.

### The Sub-Patrons

---

Two friends of the same social standing and merit had a patron, on whose kindness they both relied equally. One of them did everything in his power for the man, asked for his advice on every occasion—in short, he demonstrated his devotion in every possible way. But only to the master! He paid no mind to the servants. A single impulse from the Great Man, he thought, would "electrify" the whole crowd <note>The author uses a scientific metaphor here: just as an electrical shock travels through a chain of people, the character believes the patron's favor would automatically flow down to everyone else in the house without any effort on his part</note>.

--- Page 118 ---
<meta>This page continues the moral fable titled "The Sub-Patrons" from page 41. While the first friend focused his devotion exclusively on the master of the house, this page describes the contrasting strategy of the second friend and the lesson learned from their different results.</meta>

The other man, however, also knew how to win the favor of everyone in the household through gifts and small favors. Whatever his patron <note>The German term "Gönner" implies a benefactor or someone in a position to grant professional or social advancement</note> intended for him, he received immediately; he was even able to take advantage of the patron's unpredictable moods. This was because he was kept informed about everything. By contrast, hardly the most insignificant trifle ever reached his friend.

It grieved the first friend that he received so few substantial benefits from the favor of the man he served so zealously; however, it never occurred to him that the fault lay solely with the intermediaries <term>Zwischenpersonen: middle-men or gatekeepers, in this case the household servants and staff</term>. Then, one evening, he happened to meet an acquaintance. To him, he lamented his sad situation.

"Look here," the acquaintance interrupted, holding up a lantern he happened to have with him, "and see how it is with you! Through these strips of tin <note>The speaker is referring to the metal frame of a traditional pierced-tin or paneled lantern; the metal blocks the light while the clear panels let it through</note>, the glimmer of light pierces through only in tiny dots; but through the glass beside it, it shines in its full brilliance."

The Eternal One forgave him where he had erred, and heavenly bliss became his portion.

In the enjoyment of this bliss, he awaits the Day of the Great Judgment at the throne of God. Then, in the presence of all nations, the All-Just One will let his enemies accuse him and let him defend himself; and even those enemies shall then cry out: He was innocent! Let bliss be his reward!

### The Human.

### An Allegory.

---

Nature, a very wealthy widow, had a son, the Spirit <note>Original: "Geist." In this context, it refers to the intellect or the non-material soul of a human being.</note>, whom she loved most tenderly of all her children and raised with the greatest care. When he reached manhood, she married him to a girl who was perfectly made for him: Matter. Reason was the contribution <note>Original: "Einbringen," referring to the assets or dowry brought into a marriage contract.</note> from his side, and Will from hers; and both were very considerable. They now began the management of the household of life together. Memory became the Steward <note>Original: "Haushofmeister," the high-ranking official in charge of a noble household.</note>. Under him stood five quick, industrious servants: the Senses. The most fortunate harmony appeared as the first benefit of these arrangements, and the richest treasures of wisdom and virtue were the result. Regarding this

<summary>This page introduces an allegory of the human condition, personifying the components of a person—such as Spirit, Matter, Reason, and the Senses—as members of a well-ordered noble household. It describes the harmonious union of mind and body as the source of wisdom and virtue.</summary>
<keywords>allegory, human nature, Spirit and Matter, Reason, Will, Memory, Senses, Enlightenment philosophy, household imagery</keywords>

--- Page 123 ---
<meta>This page continues the allegory of "The Human," detailing the internal management of the household (representing the human soul and body) and its eventual decline into vice and disorder. It follows from the previous page's introduction of the Spirit (husband) and Matter (wife).</meta>

three of the most experienced men received the supervision: Ambition <note>Original: *Ehrtrieb*, literally the "drive for honor."</note>, Utility, and Pleasure. Thus, the finest order reigned everywhere. The husband commanded his wife as a friend; she obeyed him by her own choice <note>Original: *Willkühr*. In this context, it refers to the faculty of the Will acting voluntarily rather than modern "arbitrariness."</note>. The servants served out of inclination. The steward <note>Referring to Memory, who was introduced on the previous page.</note> was frugal, and yet he appeared only generous. The sub-overseers prescribed this and that, and yet they seemed only to give suggestions <note>Original: *Winke*, literally "nods" or "hints."</note>.

But alas! the happiness of even this marriage was not to last! She became vain, and he a debauchee <note>Original: *Schwelger*, meaning someone who indulges excessively in luxury or gluttony.</note>. The servants now began making mistakes here and there; their superior forgot to punish them, and often did not even notice. Ambition resisted this ruin the longest; but at last, he had to give way, and a Pandora with her evils <note>A reference to the Greek myth of Pandora, whose box/jar released all the miseries of the world.</note> took his place. Soon, Utility also departed of its own accord. Pleasure, enfeebled by age and illness, was already dead while still alive. Wealth was squandered, honor was lost, the head was a wasteland,

### The Women’s Oracle.

---

At the most recent Imperial Diet <note>Original: *Reichstag*. The supreme deliberative assembly of the Holy Roman Empire where various estates met to discuss legal and political matters.</note>, the female sex had submitted a petition to the following effect: they had gradually devised a great many rules of wisdom, household remedies, omens, and ghost stories, collected during the long nights of the previous winter at the spinning wheel, and now wished to make them known to the public under the title: **The Women’s Oracle**. They therefore requested from the highly wise assembly a license for censorship <note>In the 18th century, "Censur" was the official review process required to obtain legal permission to print a book.</note> and exclusive printing privileges. Every art of persuasion <note>Original: *Suada*, a Latin-derived term referring to the Roman goddess of persuasion; used here to describe the power of eloquent or seductive speech.</note>, from the unveiled bosoms of rosy girls to the heavenward-glancing eyes of the little grandmothers, was employed. And yet, many Estates of the Empire <note>Original: *Reichsstände*. The entities (such as princes, counts, and cities) that had the right to vote in the Diet.</note> found the demand inadmissible and audacious. The ignorant ones clearly knew nothing of feminine pansophy <note>Original: *Pansophie*, meaning "universal wisdom." The author uses this high-minded term for "all-encompassing knowledge" with a touch of irony to describe the collection of domestic folk-lore.</note>! Others demanded that the collection

---

### He is Right.

---

Biederfinn <note>A name likely derived from "Biedersinn," meaning an upright, honest, or integrous mind.</note> had criticized some villainies of the Great <note>original: "Grofsen." Referring to the high-ranking nobility or those in positions of power.</note>. This entangled him in a painful legal process. He had to appear before the court. The points of the indictment were presented to him, and he acknowledged them. "Here I stand," he added, "God help me! I cannot do otherwise." <note>Biederfinn is quoting Martin Luther’s famous 1521 defense at the Diet of Worms, framing his social criticism as a matter of unshakable religious and moral conscience.</note> The votes were collected. The First: "He is right—the chatterbox! But, without being meddlesome, he could not have learned of it, and, without danger to us, he must not know it. Therefore, away with him." The Second: "He is right; but must posterity know our weaknesses?" The Third: "He is right; yet it is always very reckless to offend the first men in the State." The Fourth: "He is right; however, who likes to see their own ugliness depicted?" The Fifth: "He is right; but what a daredevil <note>original: "Waghals." Literally "dare-neck," meaning someone who recklessly risks their life or safety.</note>

### Contribution to Contemporary History.

**The Judges:** And when, and how, and from whom, oh plaintiffs, did these hives once become yours?

A praiseworthy confession! the beetles smiled scornfully; in that case, we were only avenging the disgrace and innocence of the bees upon you!

But, asked the judges, do the bees now possess their hives once more, and was their honey restored to them?

Neither of the two, the wisest beetle now took the floor; for what purpose would we have administered justice, if the ill-gotten goods we discovered were not to be the reward for our own efforts?

### The Sedge and the Grapevine.

"I have pity for you," boasted the tall sedge <note>original: "Riedgras"; a type of coarse marsh grass or reed.</note> at the edge of a swamp to the grapevine on the adjacent hill. "Your soil is so parched; and even if you do push out a sprout, man comes along and cuts it off. Whatever he leaves behind serves only to chain you down. Even your roots are disturbed by his hoe <term>Karst</term> <note>A heavy, two-pronged mattock or hoe traditionally used in vineyards to break up hard, stony soil.</note>. Look at me! What rich earth! What an abundance of sap! And how I am left undisturbed in all of it!"
