--- Page 142 ---
<meta>This page concludes the fable of "The Sedge and the Grapevine," which began on the previous page. It serves as a moral allegory comparing the short-lived, easy success of the reed to the disciplined, resilient, and ultimately fruitful life of the grapevine.</meta>

The grapevine remained silent. Summer arrived. Even the milder warmth of the early season had already withered the sedge <note>original: "Riedgras"; a coarse type of marsh grass or reed.</note>; for its roots had been sucked dry by its own overly lush stalk. Now the sun glowed more fiercely. Then the swamp dried up and—the grass was parched. The herds from the pasture trampled it into the ground.

The grapevine was accustomed to the dry soil. Seldom did a rain refresh it now, but it held the sap within itself. The green of its leaves smiled cheerfully, and the grapes gleamed lovelily from beneath them. And in the autumn, it refreshed mankind with an abundance of delicious drink; and received in return the sweetest thanks: to be loved.

If fortune <note>The German "Glück" can mean both luck and happiness; here it refers to the outward gifts of fate.</note> has bestowed its gifts upon us sparingly, and if fate should test us in many ways in the future, take heart! Brother youths <note>The address to "Brother youths" (Brüder Jünglinge) suggests this text may have been intended for a student society or a moral instructional book for young men.</note>! Let us think of the grapevine.

### The Cabal.

---

The hatred of the Cabal <note>In this context, "Cabal" refers to a secret political clique or faction plotting in the shadows.</note> toward the well-being of the state was now on the verge of breaking out into open war. Thus, it recruited. But it did not recruit in public squares, nor did it take everyone who offered themselves. It did not grant its approval to the brave, but to the deceitful <note>original: "Ränkevolle"; one who is full of schemes, intrigues, or underhanded plots.</note>; not to the one who spoke loudly, but to the whisperer; not to the man with an open brow and firm step, but only to the creeper with his gaze cast toward the earth. The more despicable the soul, the more cringing the appearance, the more depraved the creature in general: the higher his rank in its favor. Whoever recommended himself to the Cabal in this way was taken into a dark cave, reached by a trapdoor.

Here, it whispered a few more questions to him. For example: Whether he [hated] the patriots of the country’s—

They swore allegiance to the banner of self-interest and received the armor of malice.

And where—some confidants now asked the mistress—where shall we draw up our battle lines? Nowhere! was the answer.

Yet! how could I expect anything from the rabble? he said, to hide his embarrassment, and went toward a Gymnasium <note>A type of German secondary school that emphasizes classical learning and the humanities.</note>. ### The Naked Truth.

---

Clad in the garment of nature, Truth walked among humanity. Already the nakedness of her flawless body and her firm, ever-steady stride reminded everyone she encountered of their own stains and missteps; but her rebukes did so even more loudly. People, however, took great offense at this, and insults were the mildest and rarest punishment for such insolence. Usually, the shape of whatever tools or weapons each person happened to have with them at the moment left their mark upon her body. Scars and welts already covered her when she now encountered her old friend **Aesop** <note>Aesop (c. 620–564 BCE) was a legendary Greek storyteller credited with a number of fables now collectively known as Aesop's Fables.</note>. "Pitiable one!" he called out to her from afar, "why did you venture among baboons and monkeys? <note>original: "Paviane und Meerkatzen." In 18th-century German satire, these primates were often used as metaphors for people who mimic behavior without understanding or who act with irrational cruelty.</note> One more day and you will be a cripple!" "But my dear friend! What am I to do?" was her

## Fragments.

### First Collection.

---

Fools and villains, whose timid ears ring at the sound of truth, do you compare satirists to dogs? Let that be forgiven you. To wish to rob you of every means of revenge—even those methods which only you would employ—would be cruel. But you are triple-fools! for wanting so badly to put them on chains. The nobler the breed of dog, the more terrifying a chain makes him to thieves and murderers. P. 238. <note>The author likely refers to a specific source or edition on page 238, possibly regarding the classical role of the satirist as a "watchdog" of morality who "bites" at vice.</note>

High above, reaching toward the stars, stands the castle of wisdom. From there, one surveys the entire surface of the earth below, and above, one hears the harmony of the spheres <term>The "harmony of the spheres" is an ancient philosophical concept (musica universalis) suggesting that the movements of celestial bodies create a divine, mathematical music.</term>. The scholars know this. So, they climb up some little hill and imagine they are looking down through the clouds, and—it is merely the treetops that [hide] the earth [from them]... <meta>The text cuts off here; the catchword "Erde" indicates the sentence continues on the next page, likely describing how the scholars' perspective is obstructed by their own limited reach.</meta>

--- Page 153 ---
<meta>This page continues the "Fragments" section from page 74, completing the aphorism regarding scholars who mistake their limited perspective for true wisdom before moving on to observations on human contentment and the corruption of collective society.</meta>

...veil the earth. They stand too close to the clouds to be able to observe the earth; and to hear the heavens, they are too little removed from the dust. P. 58. <note>original: "Erde verhüllen." This completes the thought from the previous page: scholars who climb "little hills" are blinded by the very clouds they seek to surpass, while remaining too grounded in worldly "dust" to achieve true spiritual insight.</note>

In the great hospital <term>The German "Spital" historically refers not just to a clinic, but to a charitable institution or infirmary for the poor and infirm, suggesting here that the world is a place of universal human suffering and need.</term> of the world, nourishment is weighed on a scale. And wonderful is the equality of proportions. No matter how much the one pan of hardship contains, the one who weighs knows exactly how much imagination, sensuality, mental power, force, wealth, honor, sleepiness, or appetite to put in the other, so that everyone considers their own portion the best; until the monster Envy comes creeping to him. From her, he learns to compare himself with others, to criticize the justice of the distributor, and to make himself unhappy. P. 122.

Let the atheist be isolated and he prays; the bloodsucker <note>A metaphorical term for a parasite, usurer, or extortionist.</note> and he is just; the fool and he plays the thinker. But let them unite, and—piety, justice, and science are crimes worthy of death. P. 64.

--- Page 154 ---
<meta>This page continues the series of aphorisms and moral observations. It transitions from the critique of social corruption and collective vice on the previous page to reflections on the "Law of Wisdom" and a satirical comparison between ambitious courtiers and the mentally ill.</meta>

As you teach, so act; as you see, so judge; as you read, so understand; as you love, so help; as you have, so give; as you think, so speak: thus runs the **Law of Wisdom**. "What a monotonous whole life would then be!" cry its more insightful pupils; "fortunately, it is not so." Some teach, others act; some see, others judge; some read, others understand; some love, others help; some have, others give; some think, others speak. In this way, every individual purpose is achieved, and yet in the whole, what entertaining variety <term>The German "Mannichfaltigkeit" implies a rich, pleasing diversity or multiplicity, a key concept in 18th-century aesthetics and philosophy where the "variety of the world" was seen as a sign of divine or natural perfection.</term>! P. 49.

## The Triumph.

### A Seer’s Vision of Posterity.

---

Freedom and innocence were finally defeated, and Despotism made a Roman entry into the city of seven hills <note>A traditional reference to Rome, the historical seat of both the Roman Empire and the Papacy.</note>. It was on the day of the blood-debt festival of the human race. A long procession of Druids and Assassins went out to meet the victor.

The procession arrived. At the front were paintings of St. Bartholomew's Day <note>Original: "Bartholomäusnacht." Refers to the 1572 massacre of French Protestants (Huguenots) in Paris.</note>, scenes from the history of the Edict of Nantes <note>The 1598 decree granting rights to Protestants, the revocation of which in 1685 led to massive persecution.</note>, and from the conversion of America, *autos-da-fé* <note>Original: "Autodafees." Portuguese for "acts of faith"; the ritual of public penance for heretics during the Inquisition, often ending in execution.</note> and the Duke of Alba’s <note>A Spanish general notorious for his brutal suppression of the Dutch Revolt in the 16th century.</note> coat of arms in raised workmanship. Then came the **spoils** <term>spoils: from the Latin 'spolia', referring to the armor, weapons, or goods stripped from a defeated enemy.</term> of freedom and various booty from the huts of innocence, all washed in blood. Rising high out of the heap now were the gifts of the dark underworld: the consecrated swords.

<note>Vocabulary: Despotism, St. Bartholomew's Day Massacre, Edict of Nantes, America, Autos-da-fé, Duke of Alba, Spoils</note>

<summary>This page presents a dark, allegorical vision of Despotism entering Rome in a triumphal procession, surrounded by symbols of historical religious and colonial violence. It frames tyranny as a ritualistic victory built upon the blood and "spoils" of the innocent.</summary>

<keywords>Despotism, allegory, Roman triumph, St. Bartholomew’s Day Massacre, Edict of Nantes, Spanish Inquisition, Duke of Alba, political violence, religious persecution</keywords>

A vast herd of brave lions, watchful dogs, industrious bulls, and noble steeds was driven along behind them, as a sacrifice to the Goddess of Despondency. 

With murder in their eyes and blood on their fists, the faithful servants of Despotism marched along. Many a land in Europe would blush were I to name those whom my gaze recognized here. ### Us and Them.

**The Past** <note>original: "Die Vorzeit", literally "primeval times" or "antiquity," here personified as a figure of traditional virtue.</note> encountered the **Present**. The former, a dignified matron, was dressed with taste yet very simply. In her granddaughter, however, she found a frivolous, flirtatious girl, whose colorful, gaudy finery struck her just as strongly as her voluptuous posture, which seemed to have studied all the arts of seduction and invited every kind of pleasure. She could not restrain herself from expressing her dissatisfaction. 

But nimbleness of tongue was also not the least of things to which the young girl had applied herself. "But Mother! What a lack of aesthetic sense! What rustic simplicity! How utterly old-fashioned <note>original: "altfränkisch," literally "Old-Franconian." In this period, the term was used to describe something outmoded, antiquated, or "from the old school."</note> that was spoken." Thus the stream of her speech began to pour forth, and for a long, long time it flowed unstoppably—

### Means to Greatness.

->F 4<-

**A** new Grandison <note>A reference to Sir Charles Grandison, the hero of Samuel Richardson's 1753 novel. <meta>Continuity: This page continues the response of the "new Grandison"—the archetype of the virtuous and successful man—to the petty criticisms of his peers. With a smile, the envied man heard these and similar expressions of resentment, which masqueraded as exercises in "causal philosophy" <note>original: "Cauſalphiloſophie." But at last, he began: "Then hear from my own lips which steps carried me to the Temple of Fame."

"**Neglect** formed my talents; for since no one guided me, I had to practice walking my own path. I owe my **skills** to **poverty**. She denied me sustenance unless I earned it by the sweat of my brow. **Slander** shaped my character. With a hundred eyes she watched me, and all her thousand tongues gossiped about me. I dared let the former see only what was good, so that the latter had nothing but lies to chatter. What I accomplished was deemed insignificant. Thus, **envy** kept me in restless activity <note>original: "Thätigkeit," implying industriousness and constant work.</note> through the compulsion to put my detractors to shame. The prosperity of my fortune stems from **ingratitude**. This made me suspicious of everyone, and I no longer squandered anything on..."

### The Cloak.

A <term>hypocrite</term> <note>original: *Kopfhänger*, literally "head-hanger." Various ones were shown to him, but none suited him. “I need one,” he said, “which is black on this side and white on the other, but otherwise made of the same cloth.” “Strange!” said the merchant. “Judging by your face, I must take you for a <term>respectable man</term>, <note>original: *Biedermann*. To speak plainly!—a rogue’s coat.” “But my God!” the buyer replied, smiling sweetly from beneath his thick eyebrows, “but my God! Good, dear man! Do you not know at all what kind of world we live in? You cannot get by with a single outfit! The pulpit requires one coat, the coffee house another, and the court—” <note>The text breaks here mid-word at *Gerichts-*, referring to a court of law or justice.</note>

### ->The Charitable.<-

In former times, Charity <note>The German *Mildthätigkeit* is personified here as a female figure.</note> had the custom of publicly granting her blessing to her friends once a year. She found that the number of those who laid claim to it grew ever larger, and yet the cries of the poor concerning their lack grew ever louder. Also, the public's respect for this ceremony, if not entirely lost, had significantly diminished. Therefore, on the next occasion, she held an inspection of the praise-hungry crowd beforehand. 

There she found among them people who had squandered their money on tightrope walkers, sleight-of-hand artists, alchemists <note>original: *Goldmacher* — literally "gold-makers."</note>, coffeehouse keepers, and procuresses <note>original: *Maquereusen* — a loanword from French for female pimps or brothel-keepers.</note>. "You deserve to be publicly shamed," she spoke to them, full of indignation, "but you shame yourselves; begone from my sight!" With a self-satisfied smile and intrusive chatter, a <meta>catchword: one</meta>

And we, said the others—her long-time trusted favorites—we did not come to hear our own praise, but to discover someone in need of help among the crowd of spectators at this ceremony. We can find such people elsewhere as well; let your blessing never be profaned again. — Since then, that custom has ceased.

<meta>This page marks a transition from the previous allegory concerning Charity to a new satirical or philosophical essay titled "The Theory of Practice." It discusses the overwhelming number of applicants for government positions and introduces the personified character of "Practice" to the narrative.</meta>

### The Theory of Practice.

No office or minor post <term>Aemtchen: a diminutive form of 'Amt,' implying a low-ranking or trivial government position</term> in the state is vacated before a race of candidates for it begins. What the cause of this may be—whether it is the lucky star of our century, which possesses such an abundance of capable men, or the audacity of the masses, who believe themselves so easily equal to the most important affairs, or perhaps the laziness or greed of the superiors, who thereby have the least effort and the most profit—I leave undecided.

Recently, several offices were to be filled again. Then, to the great annoyance of the Gentlemen Curators <note>original: *Curatoren* — trustees or administrative overseers of a public institution</note>, Practice herself took charge in person. She appeared:

> "Let nothing trouble you! Among you I will choose, numerous candidates! But—my prince must, like Decius <note>Decius refers to the Roman consul Publius Decius Mus, who famously sacrificed his life in battle to ensure victory for his people; here it likely suggests a leader showing himself to be a servant or sacrifice for the citizenry</note>, [show] himself to his citizens gladly <meta>catchword: gern (gladly/willingly)</meta>

<summary>The author critiques the modern obsession with seeking government offices, suggesting it stems from either an overabundance of talent or the greed of superiors. The personified figure of "Practice" intervenes in the appointment process, challenging the candidates and the prince's relationship with the public.</summary>

<keywords>bureaucracy, civil service, candidates, Practice, personification, Decius, civic duty, political satire</keywords>

--- Page 180 ---
<meta>This page continues the speech of the personified "Practice" from the previous page, where she sets high moral and professional standards for those who wish to serve the state.</meta>

be able to sacrifice himself willingly. My preacher, like the martyrs <note>original: *Blutzeugen* — literally "blood-witnesses," referring to early Christian martyrs</note> of the Church, must still teach and comfort even at the stake. My legal expert, even in the face of the executioner's axe, must with **Papinian** <note>Aemilius Papinianus (142–212 AD), a celebrated Roman jurist who was executed for refusing to write a legal justification for the Emperor Caracalla's fratricide</note> call right "right." My warrior, a second **Cocles** <note>Horatius Cocles, a legendary Roman hero who famously defended the Sublician Bridge alone against the entire Etruscan army</note>, must hold back the enemy by himself. My physician must remain a physician even during the plague; my philosopher must know how to serve as a gravedigger in case of need; and my man of letters <term>Literator: a scholar or person of wide literary learning</term> must be as important as thousands in his Syracuse <note>A reference to the scientist Archimedes, whose genius alone defended the city of Syracuse against the Roman fleet</note>. Just as I would pick out from the myriads of people who call themselves citizens and sons only that citizen who could act for the whole like **Curtius** <note>Marcus Curtius, a mythical Roman hero who sacrificed his life by leaping into a chasm to save the Republic</note>, and only that son who, during the burning of his Troy, first saved his elderly parents <note>An allusion to Aeneas, who carried his father Anchises out of the burning ruins of Troy</note>, as those worthy of the name.

Now she had ended her address and found herself alone. The candidates had all slunk away. This was exactly what she had intended. She knew the individuals she required; but these had not crowded themselves among the masses. Now she sought them out in their solitude, and with them the offices were filled.

## The Young Genius.

Lovingly in its springtime, the fortunate genius sprouted from the mild soil; its branches and leaves were beautiful, and its blossoms smelled sweet. Then they watered the lovely little tree, and pruned, supported, and fenced it in. — The child became a lively, agile, witty boy, and rewarded the care of his friends through the countless pleasure he gave them. But now he had gradually matured into a young man <term>Jüngling: a youth, typically in the transition between childhood and adulthood, often associated with budding idealism</term>. Soon his observant, inquiring gaze <note>original: *Forscherblick* — a penetrating or analytical way of looking at the world</note> boldly penetrated the conditions of society, the mutual duties and the mutual conduct of people toward one another; and there, many a gap and many an excess struck him as strange. He spoke this without restraint, and allowed himself neither to be talked into things, nor dazzled, nor subjugated, nor

frightened, nor deceived, nor bribed <note>original: *erkaufen* — literally "to buy," implying the youth could not be corrupted by financial offers</note>, nor lulled to sleep, nor fed <note>original: *verköstigen* — suggesting the use of hospitality or luxury to gain his favor</note>, nor worn down. Now the established people <note>original: *gesetzten Leute* — the senior, respectable figures of the social order</note>, whom he had unintentionally offended bitterly with his observations, very much regretted having supported him.

They feared and hated him. They deliberated how his lofty spirit <term>Geist: the combination of intellect, wit, and soul</term> could be suppressed, his sharp insight <term>Scharfblick: keen perception or analytical discernment</term> blunted, his momentum <term>Schwungkraft: the inner drive or buoyancy of a creative mind</term> paralyzed, and his warmth chilled—or at the very least, how his effectiveness could be made impossible. But all attempts, whether through kindness or through force, were fruitless. Then they entrusted the task to the monster Slander <term>Verläumdung: defamation; the act of making false and damaging statements</term>, and saw themselves satisfied.

<meta>This page transitions from the narrative of the "Young Genius" into a specific allegory titled "The New Samaritan." While the previous page described the elite's decision to use Slander against the youth, this page dramatizes that attack as a metaphorical highway robbery, echoing the biblical Parable of the Good Samaritan to illustrate the destruction of a person's reputation.</meta>

## The New Samaritan.
### An Allegory.

---

A youth intended to travel from the Residence of Good Reputation <note>original: *Residenz des guten Rufs*</note> to the Trading City of Social Life <note>original: *Handelsstadt des gesellschaftlichen Lebens*</note>. Courageous, almost carefree, he wandered along the Road of Good Conscience. Suddenly, three robbers set upon him: Slander <term>Verläumdung: the spreading of false reports to damage a reputation</term>, Envy, and Deception. They took from him all his gear of past conduct <note>original: *Geräthschaft des seitherigen Betragens* — his history of good behavior</note>, robbed him of the clothes of good testimony <note>original: *Kleider des guten Zeugnisses* — his public standing and the "proof" of his character</note>, and dealt him deep, deep wounds of lies and malice. He wrestled with the death of disgrace <note>original: *Tode der Schande* — the total loss of honor within society</note>.

Then the Fatherland <note>original: *Vaterland*</note> came down the road. It owed the wounded man many an obligation. It saw him and muttered something to itself—whether lamentations or scoldings, one could not clearly tell—and indifferently strode on.

| Key Terms | |
| :--- | :--- |
| **Samaritan** | A figure from a biblical parable who helps a stranger in need. |
| **Allegory** | A story where characters and events represent moral or political ideas. |
| **Slander** | False statements intended to damage a person's character. |
| **Envy** | Resentment or jealousy toward another's success. |
| **Deception** | The act of tricking or misleading someone. |
| **Fatherland** | One's native country, here personified as an ungrateful entity. |

Soon afterward came **Kinship**. She secretly admitted to herself that the unfortunate man might have claims upon her. With a fearful ear <note>original: *schüchternem Ohr* — literally a "shy ear," implying she is listening intently only to ensure she avoids being asked for help</note>, dreading that he might beg her for assistance at any moment, she tiptoed past him in total silence.

She was followed by **Upbringing**; an upright woman who had formerly done her very best for the unfortunate youth, but who was now somewhat suspicious and harsh. "Had you forgotten me so completely that these punishments for your excesses had to befall you? Let this serve for your improvement!" she scolded <note>original: *eiferte* — suggests a self-righteous or zealous reprimand</note>, and she left him lying there.

Melancholy <term>Wehmuth: a state of wistful sadness or pensive gloom</term> would have almost completely sapped the poor man’s remaining strength; but his dim eyes caught sight of **Acquaintanceship** in the distance. He summoned every ounce of his remaining strength and whimpered for mercy. But the friends, in their high spirits, were just then singing a little song about the enjoyment of life <note>original: *Lebensgenuß*</note>, and because of their noise, they failed to hear <note>original: *verhörten* — to miss hearing something due to other distractions</note> his faint pleading.

The sigh that he sent after them was, he hoped, to be his last. Then came the

<meta>This page concludes the allegory of "The New Samaritan." Having been ignored by his own country, family, and former teachers, the "Youth" (representing a person of talent or genius) is finally rescued by a personification of the "Foreign Land."</meta>

**Foreign Land** <note>original: *Ausland* — personified here as the "Good Samaritan" figure from the parable, representing how a prophet or genius is often better received abroad than in their own country</note>. It had already heard of him elsewhere, and no ulterior motives <note>original: *Nebenbetrachtung* — literally "side-considerations"; secondary thoughts that might cloud judgment</note> hindered it from recognizing the true cause of his misery. It dressed his wounds, aided his recovery in every possible way, and returned to him the belongings <note>original: *Geräthſchaft* — tools, equipment, or apparatus; in this allegory, likely signifying the youth's intellectual or creative tools</note> that had been snatched back from the robbers. Soon he found himself once again in the most flourishing prosperity <term>Wohlstand: a state of thriving, both financially and in terms of general well-being</term>, and through him, the Foreign Land also became wealthier and more esteemed.

Now his **Fatherland** <note>original: *Vaterland*</note> boasted that it had sired him; his **Kinship** was proud of him; "he actually owes all his good fortune to me!" said **Upbringing**; and **Acquaintanceship** bragged everywhere of their former intimacy. But not one of them admitted—though it was as true as any of their other claims—that had his welfare once depended solely on help from them, he would have been lost long ago!

---

| Vocabulary | |
| :--- | :--- |
| **Foreign Land** | Representing the reception of talent or virtue in a place where one has no prior history or prejudice. |
| **Fatherland** | Representing one's native country, which often only values its citizens after they have achieved success elsewhere. |
| **Upbringing** | Here shown taking credit for the youth's success while having previously abandoned him in his time of need. |
| **Prosperity** | The result of the youth's restored health and the return of his "tools" (his talents or resources). |
| **Kinship** | The relatives who are quick to claim a connection to success but slow to offer aid during failure. |

<summary>This page concludes an allegorical tale where a talented youth is saved by a "Foreign Land" after being abandoned by his own people, highlighting the hypocrisy of those who only claim credit for a person's success after they have already succeeded.</summary>

### The Medicines.

---

Various diseases <term>Krankheiten: illnesses or ailments</term> devastated our city. The physicians <term>Aerzte: doctors or medical practitioners</term> did not lack in attentiveness and untiring zeal; plenty of medicine <term>Arznei: drugs or medicinal treatments</term> was also consumed; and yet, recovery <term>Geneſung: the process of healing or convalescence</term> was generally delayed. People blamed now the air, now the season, now the climate <note>original: *Clima*</note>. But the sick were still not helped by this, and other physicians were hired. They investigated the illnesses, wrote prescriptions <term>Recepte: medical prescriptions</term>, saw no success, and—shook their heads.

Finally, old **Aesculapius** <note>original: *Aesculap* — the Greek god of medicine and healing, often used here as a personification of the ideal, wise physician</note> arrived. His first path was to the pharmacy <term>Apotheke: an apothecary or druggist's shop</term>. There he found very neat little boxes and vials, beautifully painted in many colors and with promising labels. He looked inside, and the medicines had the proper color, the required weight, and stood in the

The Egotist <term>Selbstling: a person who is entirely self-centered or preoccupied with their own importance</term>, who until now had been capable of everything, knew everything, and had done everything, was thrown onto his sickbed by a fever <term>Fieber</term>—to his great astonishment! He commanded the astrologers <term>Astrologen</term> to look to the heavens, and the physicists <term>Physiker</term> to observe the earth, to see what revolution <term>Revolution: here meaning a major cosmic upheaval or change in the natural order</term> was imminent. They unanimously assured him that they had noticed nothing unusual in either the heavens or the earth; everything was taking its normal course. "Oh! You observed too fleetingly," he cried; "certainly the stars are wandering astray among one another, and the earth trembles in anticipation of the things that are to come." Now he sent for the physicians <term>Aerzte</term>. He emphatically presented to them the loss that the world would face with his death: "I implore you, if the welfare of humanity <term>Menschheit</term> lies at your heart, then make me as quickly as possible—"

### The Pilgrims.

It is very difficult to happily traverse the rough path of life! It is all the more difficult because the wisest caution <term>Vorsicht</term> of the wanderer—even if it is only a little too meticulous—can so easily cause him to miss his purpose.

Some stride along in beautiful white robes, measuring and counting their steps, and their eyes never look up from the earth. Because of this, they are soon seized by dizziness <term>Schwindel: here referring to a mental or spiritual vertigo caused by over-focusing on minute details</term>; they strike against the smallest pebble, fall to the ground, and—the beautiful robe is rendered unrecognizable by filth. Others run so swiftly, unconcerned with dust, clothing, or obstacles; they often land on gravel and thorns, but their stride only quickens as they pass over them; they have no leisure for dizziness and they reach the goal. Others have a sharp eye, which is unable to limit— <note>original: "einzuſchrän-"; the text breaks off here at the end of the page and would likely continue as "einzuſchränken," meaning to limit or restrict oneself.</note>

| **Caution** | *Vorſicht* |
| **Wanderer** | *Wandrer* |
| **Dizziness** | *Schwindel* |
| **Goal** | *Ziel* |

It is difficult to wander the path of life, but not impossible.

### The Outlawed Vice.

---

### Hercules Among Us.

---

**H**ercules recently came down from Olympus to free the earth once more from monsters. Most destructive for mankind were despotism, sophistry <note>Sophistry: the use of clever but false arguments, especially with the intention of deceiving</note>, and hypocrisy, which devastated the lands that were formerly ruled by sovereignty, wisdom, and philanthropy <note>original: "Menschenliebe" — literally "love of humans"</note>. The hero had already defeated many smaller monsters, their brood; now he prepared to attack them as well with all his gathered strength. But they had already entrenched themselves in the dark fortress of ignorance.

# The World Beyond.

---

**A**s diverse as they are just, such are the fates of men beyond the valleys of death! Often, the guilty mortal departs from the earth without having received the reward <note>In this context, the author uses "reward" (Lohn) ironically to mean the just punishment or retribution for one's actions.</note> for his deeds. But even if he must leave everything behind here, he still takes his face and form with him into the realm of shadows; the most precise accusers for the searching eye of an Aeacus <note>In Greek mythology, Aeacus was a king of Aegina renowned for his justice, who became one of the three judges of the dead in the Underworld.</note>. One glance at them! and—their fate is decided.

All this I heard and saw recently, guided by my guardian angel, in the vision of a dream.

I saw tyrants as slaves to the most headstrong masters; a pretender to universal knowledge <note>original: "Vielwisser"—someone who claims to know everything; a "know-it-all."</note> without a witness; a vain man whom no soul sees or hears or even speaks of; weaklings <note>original: "Weichlinge"—referring to those who lived lives of pampered luxury or lacked moral fortitude.</note> who are frozen in eternal frost. A liar read the record of the

O! It is a pitiful thing not to be able to go astray. To be able to play tragic roles on the **stage of experience** is, unfortunately, a privilege of the first among men <note>German: *Ersten der Menschen*, referring to those of exceptional character, spirit, or social standing who dare to take risks</note>. The person of dull senses and weak strength remains stuck to the dust—and must do so—and loses himself unnoticed in the dust. Nature gave those former souls momentum; self-cultivation <note>German: *Selbstbildung*, a key Enlightenment concept referring to the lifelong process of refining one's own intellect and character</note> gave them strength; fortune <note>German: *Glück*, which in German carries the dual meaning of "luck" and "happiness"</note> gave them courage; and admiration gave them boldness. They rise up from the earth, soar beneath the heavens, now setting down upon some charming hill; in an instant they fly among the stars; they hover carelessly up and down; and now—they strike against a rock that was either unnoticed or held in contempt. The wise man shudders at their fall and sighs: For mortals, how closely the path to the highest happiness borders the deepest abyss of misfortune! P. 87.

### From the Prefaces.

I was a quick, fiery youth; and my destiny brought me into many ever-changing circumstances. From my early years, the bonds of traditional customs in matters where not custom, but right <note>German: *Recht*, which can mean "justice," "law," or "inherent right"</note> was the most important question, became burdensome and soon intolerable to me. I strove to cast them off; but the burden was too heavy for my strength. For in itself, my strength was too weak; and it could not be enhanced through emulation <note>German: *Nacheiferung*, suggesting that simply following others' examples was not enough to gain the strength needed for true independence</note>. Everyone around me was quite content in their cage. To this was added the respect for the name of Christian—for with it one conceals every defect—and the glory of our age, which is supposedly the first among all in perfection and happiness; and finally, the expectation of the approaching Last Judgment <note>German: *Weltgericht*</note>. ---

With shame and remorse, I look back on every judgment I have ever passed on any human being. Whether I was right now and then, I do not know; but this I do know: that I very often deceived myself completely.

### The Judgment of Phoebus.

Everywhere one heard the complaints of mortals regarding life and its institutions. Hope had indeed been given for a general reformation <note>original: *Generalreformation*; a term suggesting a total systemic overhaul of society to return it to a "golden age" or state of perfection</note>, which was meant to restore the state of the whole to its original perfection: but the task was found to be too great and difficult; hope vanished, and people now sought individual, special ways to patch individual cracks, and thus at least somewhat remedy the evils.

Accordingly, Phoebus <note>The Greek god Apollo, here serving as a personification of reason and divine oversight</note>, the supreme overseer of the globe, decreed the thirtieth of February of the current year <note>original: *anni currentis*; the author uses a non-existent date (February 30) to signal that this story is a satirical or impossible fiction</note> as the date upon which all those to whom social conditions <note>original: *gesellschaftlichen Verhältnisse*; refers to the structures of community, class, and law</note> had become a burden should appear before his throne. There they would learn the means to remedy these defects. The effect of this generally ex- <note>The text breaks off here; the catchword *freu-* at the bottom indicates the next page begins with a word like *erfreulichen* (joyful/pleasant).</note>

It marks a transition from general philosophical reflection to a satirical narrative about the difficulty of systemic reform.</summary>

<meta>Continuity check: The previous page ended with the announcement of a "General Reformation" by Phoebus (Apollo) on the fictional date of February 30th, leaving off at the word "joyful" (erfreulichen).</meta>

joyful news was expressed in various ways. People leaped for joy at the goodness of Phoebus; they yearned greatly for that happy day; each person leaned firmly upon the righteousness of their own cause and already threatened their opponent with every kind of evil.

When the day of blessing finally dawned, there streamed together—not a multitude, not an army—an entire world streamed together, representing that chaos about which poets fable so much and so monstrously. Phoebus found this too disorderly and had the crowd divided into classes, just as human precision and custom had introduced them in times past.

He granted precedence to the lords of the world, the monarchs. They were to be the first to bring forward their complaints. Their spokesperson, Niccolò Machiavelli <note>Niccolò Machiavelli (1469–1527), the Italian philosopher famous for *The Prince*; here he acts as the symbolic representative of cold, calculated statecraft</note>, thus complained in their name primarily about two things: the **Word of God** and the **Conscience**. The former troubled them with its brazen voice from without, the latter from within; and both disturbed the enjoyment of their happy unrestraint <note>original: *Uneingeschränktheit*; in this context, it refers to absolute power or the lack of any legal or moral boundaries</note>.

They wished to strike a deal with him so that they would never be forced to depart from this life.

After the masters came the slaves—those petty grandees—who, according to the complaint of Sejanus <note>Lucius Aelius Sejanus (20 BC – 31 AD), the ambitious and later executed confidant of the Roman Emperor Tiberius; here he represents the fragility of the status of political favorites</note>, were displeased that they, like the planets, shone only in borrowed light. "I marvel at your petulance," Phoebus replied, "for you have plenty of your own! It has long been known how well you provide for yourselves against the clouds while the sun is still shining. You have piled up fine treasures; only I fear—"

The <term>dicasteries</term> <note>original: *Dikaſterien*; high courts of justice or administrative bodies</note> desired all written laws to be abolished, so that they might draw all justice from the shrine of their own heads and consciences. Phoebus excused his hesitation by saying that one could rely too little on worm-eaten wood. The <term>consuls</term> <note>High-ranking magistrates or government officials</note>, through Mark Antony, requested permission to be allowed to powder themselves with the "flour" of the <term>fiscus</term> <note>original: *Fiſcus*; the state treasury or public coffers</note>; they argued that one should not hold it against them, just as one does not blame millers for being covered in flour dust. "Just take care," [Phoebus replied], "that when your coat is eventually dusted off, your back does not have to feel the cane at the same time." Then the <term>syndics</term> <note>original: *Syndici*; legal representatives or administrative officials of a city</note> appeared. ### A Tale of the Year 2440.

---

It is well known that our age found what all centuries had sought; though always with a futile expenditure of resources and human lives. The lands at the South and North Poles have been discovered. <meta>The previous page ended with "interest in the unfortunate fate of such," leading into "many millions" on this page.</meta>

of many millions. People hurried to provide them with all the resources of civilization and taught them how to use them. The more powerful members of these nations very quickly made the most successful progress toward Enlightenment. Compelling proof of this was their behavior toward the common people. Formerly, they had served the people as chosen leaders; now, they became their tyrannical despots. Belief, inquiry, commanding, obeying, feasting, fasting, living, and dying—everything now depended on their every whim.

Resistance against them was all the more difficult the longer it took to realize that such resistance was necessary. The strength of the people had already been extremely weakened. Yet, through increased prudence and effort, that strength was restored. The people now fought against their oppressors and triumphed. The yoke was broken, and the people became as free as they had been in the past. A satirical term for a prolific hack writer who churned out mass-market books specifically for the major German book fairs (Messen) in Leipzig or Frankfurt.</note></term>—that I can no longer bear.

### He Alone.

**E**goism <note>The author personifies "Egoism" (Self-Interest) as a character here, reflecting an 18th-century allegorical style common in moralized political critiques.</note>, distinguished neither by birth and even less by talents, had cunningly seized a favorable moment and was now the prince's factotum <term>factotum</term><note>From the Latin *fac totum* ("do everything"); a person employed to do all kinds of work or a high-ranking official who manages all of a ruler's affairs.</note>. Not only did his flatterers tell him that he was the head, heart, mouth, and hand of the regent: the truth of this testimony was sighed over by the entire country. What could never be fully accomplished, even if managed by several people, was achieved by him through a single: "So I will it!" Whether he had intended this, I do not know; but in short order, the very marrow of the country was his. His power was now terrifying to his master, his pride tiresome to his friends, and the yoke of oppression unbearable to the people. He, however, sat upon the never-wavering throne of highest fortune;

<note>original vocabulary: Egoismus (Egoism/Self-interest), Factotum (Factotum), Fürst (Prince), Regent (Regent/Ruler), Bedrückung (Oppression)</note>

So it seemed to him, and he had not noticed that his throne, built by him alone, was a house of cards <note>original: *Kartenbau*; an idiom used then as it is now to describe a structure or scheme that is deceptively fragile and liable to collapse at any moment.</note>, and guarded by him alone, had become food for woodworms. The rotting scaffold collapsed, and the limbs of the mutilated corpse found their grave in the ruins. Meager was the pleasure derived from his misdeeds; immeasurable are their avenging torments in the next world: for a short time his fame rang out; eternally his shame echoes.

# The Uniter of Religions.

An augur <note>a priest in ancient Rome who interpreted the will of the gods by observing the flight of birds</note>, a rabbi, a dervish <note>a member of a Muslim religious order known for their vows of poverty and ritual dance</note>, and a mass priest <note>original: *Meſsprieſter*; a Catholic priest authorized to celebrate the Eucharist</note> met—Heaven knows how!—in one place. To the first glance that each cast upon the others followed accusations of stupidity and deceit, which each hurled back at the others. Only, none of them believed that these accusations applied to themselves. The pagan, therefore, had to be reminded of his mythology, the Jew of the Talmud <note>the central text of Rabbinic Judaism containing legal and ethical discussions</note>, the Muslim of the Quran, and the papist <note>original: *Päpſtler*; a historical, often derogatory term for a Roman Catholic</note> of the legends of the saints. Each found three of these books to be utterly absurd and malicious, and the very one that He himself excepted, his neighbor placed at the top of the list of errors of the human spirit.

At last, a stranger offered himself as an arbiter. A man, God-fearing like a pagan, honest like a Jew, gentle like

...like a Turk <note>a historical term often used to refer to a Muslim, particularly within the Ottoman Empire</note>, and simple like a monk; in short, a Jesuit, and here, by virtue of the latest modification of his Order, a Uniter of Religions. He wanted to demonstrate each of the four systems irrefutably as the only true one, and then unite them all. In appearance, this was a monstrous contradiction; but for such a mind, it was the most natural and easiest thing in the world, if only he had been allowed to carry it out. However, his General <note>referring to the Superior General, the head of the Jesuit Order</note> forbade it; (and what other leader could the Order, which slinks in the darkness, have but the Prince of Darkness? <note>a common epithet for the Devil</note>) It was not because the General lacked confidence in the man's powers! — he knew them to be too great for that.

"Those sects," the General wrote to him, "have so much in common that a thinker of the Enlightenment <term>Aufklärer: a proponent of the Enlightenment movement which emphasized reason and individual logic over tradition</term> might well be able to bring them into one. ### The Merits of Poverty.

Abundance solemnly distributed gifts. One received this, the other that. To one he gave power, to another wisdom; to some, physical strength; to many, money; to many others, fame. Poverty had to wait until the very end; and then, at last, he poured the meager remains of his cornucopia <term>Füllhorn: the "horn of plenty," a symbol from classical mythology representing an endless supply of food and drink</term> into her lap. She accepted it with a friendly expression; thanked him as sincerely as ever, and, without announcing her gift, she went away.

--- Page 240 ---
<meta>This page continues the allegorical tale "The Merits of Poverty" from page 109, describing the public's reaction to the personification of Poverty and the introduction of Socrates to interpret her behavior.</meta>

Whoever was idle and curious enough for the task began to make conjectures <note original: "Muthmafsungen">speculations or guesses</note>. Some guessed she was driven by industriousness, others by indolence <term>Indolenz: a state of habitual laziness or a lack of concern; here implying she was too lazy to follow social rules</term>, and others by hope. The more closely she was observed, the more confused the observers became by this strange woman. She entered churches and inns; she went into the palace of the minister and into the workshop of the craftsman; she went to the former just as she went to the latter, and to the latter just as the former—completely without ceremony. Neither formal bows nor the expectation of tips <note original: "Trankgelder">literally "drink-money," a common 18th-century term for small gratuities given for service</note> slowed her down. If she was present at a meal, she sat down and ate, and it tasted excellent to her. Nevertheless, she did not first quarrel with her neighbor over social rank <note>In the 18th century, the "order of precedence" at a table was a matter of intense social importance; Poverty’s disregard for this shows her freedom from status-seeking</note>, and no one had to coax her to take her food. Finally, she slept through the night so softly, so soundly, and so securely that her more "honored," "wiser," and "wealthier" neighbors found this state of affairs entirely incomprehensible.

They presented this riddle to Socrates <note>The famous Greek philosopher is often used in Enlightenment-era fables as a personification of objective, rational wisdom</note>. "Let us see!" he said, "What is our lot <note original: "Loos">share, portion, or fate</note> in the things that strike us about her? For us..."

### The Printing Office.

---

The entire neighborhood had long since complained that the printing office kept in its house a mass of useless, wicked rabble consisting of idlers <note original: "Pflastertretern">literally "pavement-treaders"; a derogatory term for lazy people who wander the streets with no purpose</note> and those guilty of high treason <term>Majestätsbeleidigern: individuals who commit lèse-majesté, or an offense against the dignity of the ruling monarch</term>, brooding thinkers, pickpockets, and heretics; and *) dogs that ate human brains— <note>The text breaks off here mid-word "fräf-", likely "fräßen" (ate/devoured).</note>

*) See the copperplate frontispiece to Blumauer’s parodied *Aeneid*. <note>Aloys Blumauer (1755–1798) was a famous Austrian satirist whose "travesty" of Virgil's *Aeneid* was a landmark of the Josephinian Enlightenment, using humor to critique the Church and social institutions.</note>

<summary>This page concludes an allegory where Socrates identifies freedom and health as the true rewards of poverty, then begins a satirical description of a printing office as a den of radical thinkers.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the personified defense of the "Printing Office." ...devoured <note>original: "fräfsen" — completing the thought from the previous page: "dogs that ate [devoured] human brains."</note>—a gathering of creatures from whom, the shame notwithstanding, the city had the worst to fear. The Defendant <note>The "Defendant" is the personified Printing Press or Office.</note> always kept an open table, where the most distinguished people of both clerical and secular status gathered. Foreigners also enjoyed her hospitality <note original: "Gastfreiheit">A term for hospitality, often implying a generosity towards travelers and radical thinkers.</note>. Generally, she was held in high favor; and she was well enough aware of this to be able to ignore that yapping <note>A satirical reference to the "barking" of critics and censors.</note> for a long time. If she did occasionally answer, she demanded that the use and abuse made of her leniency be weighed against one another, and then—to be just.

Regardless of this, she finally saw herself compelled to provide a legal defense <note original: "gerichtliche Verantwortung">Literally "judicial accountability"; a formal legal response to charges.</note>. She did so as follows:

"The harshest complaint, regarding those dogs so hungry for brains, does not apply to me. Princes have forcibly quartered the pirate publishers <term>Nachdrucker: publishers who produced unauthorized reprints of books, a common and controversial practice in the 18th century before modern copyright laws</term> upon me. They are bound to the financial boards <term>Finanzcollegien: administrative bodies responsible for managing state revenues and economic policy</term> of your titles, in order to cameralistically <term>kameralistisch: relating to Cameralism, an Enlightenment-era German economic theory that focused on managing the state's resources and population to increase sovereign power and wealth</term> increase the stolen goods of the land through booty taken from the provinces of foreign lords."

<summary>The personified Printing Office defends its reputation, arguing that its hospitality to intellectuals is a virtue and blaming the state's own economic policies for the presence of "pirate" publishers.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the personified Printing Office's legal defense against charges of harboring "villains and fools" (radical thinkers and unauthorized publishers). On the previous page, she explained how the state had forced these "pirate publishers" upon her for economic reasons.</meta>

I know it; in the end, they tear even me to pieces; how then could I tolerate them of my own free will? Regarding the other points, perhaps my recognized merits <note>original: "Verdienſte" — referring to the cultural and economic contributions the printing industry provided to the state.</note> should allow me to expect more leniency. The judges looked at one another, and she—received her verdict.

### The Folk Poet.

---

**T**ruth traveled through the land in the guise of a folk poet. For every social class, she had composed a clever little song about its faults and frailties, playing along on her lyre <note>original: "Leier" — the traditional stringed instrument of ancient poets and bards, used here to suggest Truth's role as a storyteller.</note>. Thus, she had a unique song and her own style of singing for ministers and pastors, for judges and philosophers, for men and women, for the young and the old. She even had a specific song and style for the various temperaments within each class. Furthermore, she was expertly skilled in physiognomy <term>physiognomy: the historical practice of judging a person's character or personality from their outward appearance, especially the face</term>; based on her judgment of what a person truly was, she would sing one little song or another to them.

Some took delight in it; many ignored it, but by far the greater part grew angry. They threatened her, they cursed her, they beat her. Hardly had the singer

And it is indeed my calling!—If only I had not made enemies of all the social classes <note>original: "Stände" — refers to the "estates" or social ranks of society, such as the clergy, nobility, and commoners.</note>, and had not composed songs about them all!"

"That was not the mistake, good woman!" a passerby interrupted her lamentations. "You must have a little song for everyone; but—when with one person, sing the song about another; when with that person, sing the song about this one; for us, sing the song about our neighbor; for our neighbor, sing the song about us. Do this, and rest assured, your applause will be universal and your reward most bountiful."

---

## Tongue and Heart.

The heaven of happiness laughed brightly. In those times, the tongue chattered copiously and freely, calling its talk frankness and honesty <note>original: "Biedersinn" — a term for a simple, upright, and traditional integrity of character.</note>. It even found flatterers who thought this gossip—directed at everything and everyone—was something praiseworthy. 

But once, under the iron scepter of despotism <note>Despotism refers to a system where a single ruler holds absolute power, often exercised in a cruel or oppressive way.</note>, humanity had been terribly besieged by misfortune. Religion was then a mockery, justice a disgrace; a sense of honor wandered about in exile, and hunger sucked out all mental strength; covered by the corpses of friends murdered by hatred, deprivation, and grief, virtue languished, drawing its final breath. Yet, even then, the heart had remained free from chains and unshaken in its courage.

<vocab>Tongue, Heart, Despotism, Religion, Justice, Sense of Honor, Virtue</vocab>

Now it turned toward the **unbridled tongue**. Good luck, O speaker! Unstoppable is your eloquence; nor do I wish to restrain it. ### Spirit of the School.

A layman came into the workshop of the philosophers. He found the thinkers buried in a tumult of general concepts, and there he saw the greatest revolutions of the world-system arising under their drawing pens <note>original: "Reifsfeder" — a ruling pen or drawing instrument used for precision work; here it suggests the philosophers are "drafting" the world as if it were a technical blueprint.</note>. With astonishment, he found here time and space, and substances and predicates <note>The author is listing categories of Aristotelian and Kantian philosophy—the "building blocks" of reality according to the academic "Schools" of the time.</note>; cometary orbits, the moons of Uranus <note>The discovery of Uranus in 1781 and its moons in 1787 were major scientific events of the era, often used to symbolize the expanding reach of human knowledge.</note>, lunar volcanoes, great floods, conflagrations, battles, plagues, the ruins of monarchies, reefs transformed into republics <note>original: "Freistaaten" — literally "free states," a common term for republics during the era of the French and American Revolutions.</note>, mortality tables, architectural models of cities, family groups, and humans reduced to atoms—all in a wondrous mixture, and all obedient to the whim of their creators. “All these accidental things <note>In philosophy, "accidents" are qualities that are not essential to a thing's nature. The author is mocking how philosophers dismiss the physical world as "accidental" while obsessing over it.</note> lie outside the horizon of the wise,” spoke the wise men, and yet they had dedicated head, heart, and hand solely and entirely to these accidental things. While

<summary>This page presents a satirical allegory of a layman visiting a philosophical workshop where scholars arrogantly "reconstruct" the universe using abstract categories and scientific data while claiming to be above such worldly concerns.</summary>

But suddenly, an accident startled them all back down to earth. Their servant broke—whether he dropped it, or one of the thinkers stumbled into him, I do not know—he broke the soup bowl. Certainly, no small clatter arose on Olympus <note>In Greek mythology, the home of the gods.</note> when the Titans stormed upwards; but that was merely the buzzing of beetles compared to the noise that arose in the **School of Equanimity** <note>original: "Schule der Gemüthsruh" — likely a satirical reference to Stoicism or the academic pursuit of "ataraxia" (undisturbed calmness). The author mocks how philosophers who claim to have mastered their emotions lose them instantly over a minor household accident.</note> when it became known: the soup bowl was broken!!! ## Slander

At the <term>high road<note>Original: "Heerstrafse." Literally a military road or main thoroughfare; used here as a metaphor for the public journey of life.</note></term> of life lurks a monster, multi-headed and with a poisonous tooth, which prepares ruin for the wanderer, for itself, and for the spectator. Its name is Slander. It furiously attacks everyone who passes by on their <term>path of duty<note>Original: "Berufsweges." This refers to one's vocation or professional calling; the text suggests that no matter how righteous one's work is, they remain a target for gossip.</note></term>. Yet its teeth are dull; therefore, they can only graze the man of tough skin. But then it has a long tail, which it stirs around in swamps and strikes with it at him who mocks its tooth. Even if the filth does not stain his face, it at least befouls his clothes. And it knows that the <term>rabble<note>Original: "Pöbel." A derogatory term for the unrefined masses, whom the author suggests are the primary consumers and believers of slanderous rumors.</note></term> takes these stains to be traces of one's own uncleanness. It is difficult to wash them off; and to make it even harder, it so loves to befoul the back of the wanderer. For then he walks for a long time without knowing it,

## Dying.

The <term>Human Being<note>Original: "Menschwesen." A personified term for the human essence or humanity as a whole.</note></term> once boastfully praised its own highly cultivated disposition for the acquisition of all kinds of knowledge and skill. "And yet you are so extremely unteachable!" a serious voice interrupted the vain creature. It was **Death**. "For so many years I have been teaching you to know me, and the older you become, the more foreign I seem to you. Experience speaks it only too loudly: No one shudders more before me than those who must recommend me to others <note>A likely reference to the clergy or those who preach about the afterlife.</note>; no one avoids me more anxiously than those who point the way to me <note>Likely referring to physicians or those who study the boundaries of life.</note>; no one knows me less than those who describe me, and to whomever I come, they try to send me elsewhere."

The **Human**: "All other skills are natural; but dying—how completely against nature!"

<vocab>Dying, Death, Human Being, Nature, Experience</vocab>

<summary>This page features a brief dialogue between the personification of Humanity and Death, highlighting the irony that humans gain all types of knowledge yet remains willfully ignorant and fearful of their own end.</summary>
<keywords>Death, mortality, human nature, allegory, knowledge vs. experience, personification of Death</keywords>

--- Page 254 ---
<meta>This page continues the dialogue from the previous page, where Death responds to the Human's claim that dying is "against nature." Death argues that mortality is actually Nature's second fundamental law.</meta>

And yet it is her second fundamental law! one which you read on the forehead of everything that has breath, you frivolous creature! Even her first commandment: **Live!** you only follow perfectly when you are always mindful of this other one. What truly struggles against my mistress Nature are those unnatural arts <note>A likely critique of early modern medical interventions or the desperate pursuit of longevity at the cost of quality of life.</note> through which you attempt to extend her first law beyond its natural duration. You heed not a thousand torments, a thousand deaths, just to eke out what you call life. The breaking of your **body-machine** <term>body-machine<note>Original: "Körpermaschine." This term reflects the Enlightenment-era philosophical view, most famously proposed by Julien Offray de La Mettrie, that the human body functions like a complex mechanical system.</note></term> would be more beneficial to you than such a preservation.

And judge for yourself whether departing from here could really be so unnatural! Simple is the rose-garland with which wise Nature tied you to the earth: but thousand-fold and iron are the shackles with which vanity, delusion, and sensual pleasure—and whatever else those slaves of the unnatural are called—chain you to the dust. The fewer of these chains that hold you, the easier it will be for you to depart; the more of them there are,

### The Modern Kabbalah.

**A** young friend of the Enlightenment <term>Enlightenment<note>Original: "Aufklärung." This refers to the 18th-century intellectual movement that championed reason, science, and individual liberty over tradition and religious dogma.</note></term> once praised the progress of the human spirit in this century. Then he also mentioned that science <note>In the 18th century, the word "science" (Wissenschaft) was used more broadly than today, referring to any systematic body of knowledge, including philosophy or theology.</note> whose light was darkness and whose wisdom consisted of superstition. And yet! What effort the previous centuries wasted on the Kabbalah! <note>The Kabbalah is a tradition of Jewish mysticism. During the Enlightenment, it was often used by rationalists as a shorthand for obscure, irrational, or "backward" medieval thinking.</note> he sighed and shrugged his shoulders pityingly. That a science like this, which never appeared in the light of professional literature <term>literature<note>Original: "Schriftstellerei." This refers to the world of published, scholarly discourse and the public exchange of ideas.</note></term>, only rumbled in the gloomy minds of a few fools and was gone again so soon, does not surprise me;

->### The Spirit of the Fatherland.<-

---

It was known that Prosperity <term>Glücksstand<note>An allegorical personification of a nation's well-being, success, or "fortunate state."</note></term> would soon hold a magnificent banquet; and there were a great many who hoped to be present. But he invited only the sons and daughters of the fatherland: Religion, Law, Rights <term>Gerechtshame<note>An archaic term referring to the specific legal privileges, immunities, and traditional rights belonging to certain groups or the nation as a whole.</note></term>, History, Custom, and Yield <note>Original: "Ertrag." Referring here to the economic output or the literal fruits of the nation's labor.</note>.

The most resentful over her thwarted expectations was Foreign Influence <note>Original: "die Fremde." Personified here as a female guest representing foreign fashions, ideas, and political interests that often dominated local society.</note>. She had always been invited to every banquet, and was very often asked to arrange the entire affair, but here she found herself—for the first time—entirely excluded.
